# 33. III.D.5 — Domestication, Taming, Hunting & the Monster Economy

## 33.1 Taming versus domestication
Aetherian scholars, professional handlers, and experienced rural communities distinguish between:
- **taming** — an individual wild animal learns to tolerate, trust, or cooperate with sapient handlers;
- **domestication** — an animal lineage is selectively bred across generations until inherited traits make it broadly suited to living and working with sapient peoples.

A tame animal is not automatically a domesticated animal.

Some beasts are reproducibly tameable but effectively impossible to domesticate because of:
- slow or difficult breeding cycles,
- extreme territoriality,
- unusual intelligence,
- soul-compatibility requirements,
- magical/environmental requirements,
- impractical husbandry costs.

Wyverns are the flagship example: the Drakorian Enclave can conduct sophisticated selective breeding without turning wyverns into ordinary domestic stock.

## 33.2 Ordinary domestic animals remain important
Aetheria does **not** replace every familiar animal with a fantasy equivalent.

Horses, cattle, sheep, goats, pigs, dogs, cats, poultry, and similar animals remain central to agriculture, transport, companionship, pest control, and warfare where their niches fit.

Different cultures have developed their own breeds through long-term selection.

## 33.3 Native Aetherian domestic species
Aetherian societies also domesticate native fauna.

Examples already established or strongly implied include:
- **Marsh Bellowers** used for heavy transport in suitable wetlands,
- **Stonehoof Rams** with Dwarven domestic lines,
- region-specific Zakhar pack reptiles,
- Nymphic marine working animals,
- other local beasts used for hauling, tracking, food, fibre, guarding, navigation, or transport.

Crucially, **a species may be domesticated by one sapient people but not another**. Differences can arise from:
- physiology,
- sensory/communication channels,
- cultural traditions,
- local ecology,
- magical aptitude,
- inherited husbandry knowledge.

Thus one people's mundane livestock can be another people's difficult wild animal.

## 33.4 Pets and dangerous-animal regulation
Pet ownership is culturally and economically variable.

Ordinary cats, dogs, birds, and similar animals are common in many regions. Exotic or magical animals can require:
- specialist knowledge,
- money,
- permits/licences,
- suitable containment,
- proof of competence.

Governments with sufficient administrative capacity regulate dangerous exotic animals. Owners can be prosecuted when negligence allows a dangerous pet to escape or injure others.

Wyvernblooded and bonded wyverns are particularly difficult to regulate because the animal/rider pair may possess capabilities beyond normal local enforcement.

This problem becomes politically extreme in the extraordinarily rare case of a Wyvernblooded individual bonded to one of the **twelve true dragons**.

## 33.5 War beasts and working animals
Military and civic animal use extends beyond cavalry.

Possible roles include:
- cavalry,
- war dogs,
- scouts,
- messengers,
- draft and siege animals,
- pack animals,
- naval animals,
- aerial animals,
- magical detection animals,
- specialised beasts adapted to local terrain.

## 33.6 Animal welfare and soul ethics
Animal-welfare standards vary sharply between cultures.

Some societies treat working animals as valued partners. Others are practical but reasonably humane. Others are brutally utilitarian.

Because animals objectively possess souls, Aetherian philosophy and religion debate whether cruelty can inflict **soul damage** as well as physical suffering, and whether particular training methods are spiritually or morally impermissible.

Player Earth ethics may clash with local custom in both directions.

## 33.7 Hunting cultures
“Hunting” includes many very different practices:
- subsistence hunting,
- population management,
- pest control,
- noble sport,
- monster extermination,
- commercial harvesting,
- trophy hunting,
- religious hunting traditions.

No single moral or social meaning applies everywhere.

## 33.8 Hunting pressure changes ecology
Sustained hunting alters behaviour as well as population size.

Pressured animals may:
- become nocturnal,
- avoid roads,
- abandon watering sites,
- move into rougher terrain,
- alter migration routes,
- become warier of particular smells/noises,
- change group size or feeding schedules.

Experienced hunters can recognise these effects.

## 33.9 Tracking is a real discipline
Tracking is not reduced to glowing footprints.

Serious trackers use combinations of:
- tracks,
- scat,
- blood,
- hair/feathers/scales,
- feeding signs,
- vocalisations,
- broken vegetation,
- wind direction,
- weather,
- magical residue,
- knowledge of species behaviour and territory.

## 33.10 Butchery and post-kill labour
Killing an animal does not automatically convert it into abstract loot.

Pre-Lock systems may provide some convenience and harvesting assistance. Post-Lock, players must physically:
- butcher,
- skin,
- clean,
- preserve,
- package,
- transport,
- sell or process
what they kill, unless they employ skilled NPCs or other players.

The post-Lock question “Where is the loot button?” has a very simple answer: there isn't one.

## 33.11 Spoilage
Biological resources deteriorate realistically.

Examples:
- blood coagulates and decays,
- organs rot,
- hides spoil,
- venom can denature,
- magical glands lose potency,
- meat attracts scavengers and disease.

Preservation method, temperature, time, contamination, and magical treatment directly affect value.

## 33.12 Monster materials have biological reasons to matter
Monster products are useful because of their actual anatomy/magic, not arbitrary drop tables.

Valuable products may include:
- venom sacs,
- blood,
- magical organs,
- glands,
- reinforced hide,
- bone,
- horns/antlers,
- sensory structures,
- fats/oils,
- fibres,
- spores,
- eggs,
- specialised breath organs.

Different professions value different tissues.

## 33.13 Whole-carcass economics
Professional hunters usually try to use as much of a kill as practical.

Taking one rare fang and abandoning tonnes of usable meat, hide, bone, glands, and organs can be economically foolish.

A single carcass may be divided among:
- butchers,
- tanners,
- alchemists,
- physicians,
- mages,
- armourers,
- scholars,
- religious institutions,
- trophy buyers.

## 33.14 Farming dangerous fauna and true monsters
Beasts and dangerous creatures can be farmed for:
- meat,
- hides,
- eggs,
- venom,
- magical secretions,
- fibres,
- blood,
- other valuable biological products.

Success varies dramatically by species.

**True monsters remain fundamentally untameable.** Therefore “monster farming” is really a dangerous containment industry rather than conventional husbandry.

The Gorebrooder operations of Faklashar and Keralfidar are the most infamous extreme: animals are bred/contained for blood despite never becoming domesticated or safe.

## 33.15 Poaching, conservation, and rangers
Rare magical animals generate major black-market incentives.

Poachers may target:
- breeding adults,
- eggs,
- horns,
- magical glands,
- venom,
- hides,
- ritual body parts.

Conservation responses include:
- protected reserves,
- temple wardens,
- royal gamekeepers,
- professional **rangers**,
- investigators,
- anti-poaching patrols,
- seasonal hunting restrictions.

Poachers may knowingly overexploit a species even when extinction threatens the long-term income source; short-term incentives, poverty, criminal markets, and collective-action failure can outweigh ecological rationality.

## 33.16 Trophy culture
Trophies are socially contextual.

A legally hunted giant antler, an heirloom monster trophy, and the skull of an illegally killed endangered beast have very different meanings.

Some warrior cultures celebrate trophies. Others find wearing or displaying dead-animal parts distasteful, barbaric, sacrilegious, or wasteful.

## 33.17 Lock-In transformation
After Lock-In, hunting and husbandry become strategically central because player settlements require real:
- food,
- draft power,
- transport,
- leather,
- veterinary care,
- breeding programmes,
- meat preservation,
- ecological management.

Pre-Lock roleplayers who spent time farming, hunting, butchering, breeding, or animal handling may become vastly more useful than combat-focused players expected.

---

# 34. III.D.6 — Dungeons, Lairs, Migrations & Persistent Encounter Ecology

This subphase largely **formalises and consolidates earlier persistence/dungeon canon** rather than introducing an unrelated new system.

## 34.1 “Dungeon” is mostly player vocabulary
“Dungeon” is primarily a game-development/player term rather than a universal in-world category.

A location players call a dungeon may actually be:
- an abandoned fortress,
- occupied mine,
- cave ecosystem,
- ancient ruin,
- necromantic laboratory,
- tomb,
- sewer,
- underground city,
- shipwreck,
- dimensional anomaly,
- active enemy stronghold.

NPCs normally call the place what it actually is. Early in player/NPC contact, telling an NPC “I'm going to a dungeon” can produce a confused frown.

## 34.2 No universal dungeon template
There is no required sequence of:
**trash mobs → miniboss → boss → chest**.

Some locations contain a major predator. Some contain dozens of threats and no meaningful treasure. Some are empty. Some have occupants who leave while players are travelling there.

## 34.3 Non-instanced physical spaces
Normal physical dungeons are **non-instanced**.

If Guild A loots a ruin before Guild B arrives, Guild B encounters:
- altered terrain,
- opened doors,
- missing valuables,
- dead or displaced inhabitants,
- potentially Guild A itself.

Exceptional magical pocket spaces or anomalies may behave differently, but they are exceptions rather than the default logic of reality.

## 34.4 Pre-Lock “respawning” without visible pop-in
Pre-Lock creature replenishment usually occurs through simulated processes:
- migration,
- reproduction,
- replacement patrols,
- new occupants,
- magical spawning organisms,
- reanimation,
- hidden developer population-management systems.

Players generally do not watch a slain wolf simply materialise again in the same spot.

## 34.5 Post-Lock persistence
After Lock-In, developer-oriented population balancing ceases or becomes fully naturalised.

Examples:
- a cleared den remains cleared until real animals return,
- killing breeders can remove a local population for years,
- a destroyed bridge remains destroyed,
- emptied resource nodes do not simply reset.

## 34.6 Ecological and social succession
Abandoned locations change occupants over time.

Example progression:
fortress abandoned → scavengers arrive → Goblins occupy lower levels → predators follow livestock → burial chambers produce undead activity → adventurers clear Goblins → smugglers occupy the structure months later.

A “dungeon” can therefore be radically different across decades.

## 34.7 Sapient peoples occupy useful ruins
Orcs, Goblins, Ratmen, refugees, rebels, soldiers, criminals, merchants, and player guilds treat defensible abandoned structures as actual assets.

A useful empty fortress is not guaranteed to remain an adventure site simply because players once farmed it.

## 34.8 Lairs and territories
Apex creatures have both:
- one or more **lairs/resting sites**, and
- a much larger **territory/home range**.

Finding the correct cave does not guarantee that the creature is home.

Some creatures, especially large wyverns, maintain multiple resting sites.

## 34.9 Activity cycles
Dangerous fauna follows species-specific schedules driven by:
- day/night,
- dawn/dusk,
- hibernation,
- breeding season,
- weather,
- moons,
- migration,
- magical conditions.

Knowledge can therefore make encounters dramatically safer.

## 34.10 Migration as a physical event
Migrations can:
- block roads,
- attract predators,
- damage crops,
- create hunting opportunities,
- generate temporary markets,
- spread disease,
- displace monsters,
- alter trade routes.

Players can encounter them without accepting a quest.

## 34.11 True monsters migrate too
True monsters can change range in response to:
- prey collapse,
- magical conditions,
- overhunting,
- weather,
- rival death,
- territorial displacement.

Solving one province's Gorebrooder problem may simply push Gorebrooders into the next province.

## 34.12 Removing apex predators can worsen conditions
A dangerous apex creature may suppress several other dangerous populations.

Killing it can trigger:
- prey explosions,
- competitor expansion,
- new predator migration,
- disease shifts,
- vegetation damage.

“Kill the monster” is therefore not always ecologically simple.

## 34.13 Lairs are biologically/socially constructed
Lair structure follows actual behaviour.

Examples:
- Gorebrooders drag kills into feeding chambers,
- wyverns choose ventilation and launch access,
- web-building predators engineer anchors,
- Goblins build doors/traps,
- burrowing creatures destabilise rock,
- undead accumulate possessions through fragmented memory.

## 34.14 Traps have builders and purposes
Mundane traps normally make contextual sense.

A tomb trap protects a tomb. A Goblin trap protects an expected approach. A hunter's snare targets a plausible animal.

Ancient magical complexes can become stranger, but random trap tiles are not the default ecology of Aetheria.

## 34.15 Puzzles are usually practical systems
Most “puzzles” are actually:
- security systems,
- rituals,
- damaged machinery,
- linguistic problems,
- ceremonial tests,
- environmental obstacles.

Some cultures genuinely build riddle-locks, but the world is not filled with treasuries protected by arbitrary puzzle minigames.

## 34.16 Treasure has provenance
Treasure exists because something happened to put it there.

Possible origins:
- former inhabitants,
- hidden savings,
- grave goods,
- victims' possessions,
- monster collecting behaviour,
- lost cargo,
- military stores,
- criminal caches.

A wolf does not drop 14 silver pieces unless it swallowed or ate something carrying 14 silver pieces.

### Silverbelly / the wolf-gutting anecdote
One early player famously gutted a wolf and unexpectedly found swallowed coins from a prior victim. The story spread through forums and caused a wave of players to learn gutting and skinning on the theory that valuables might be inside prey.

NPC hunters found the craze deeply confusing.

## 34.17 Collecting behaviour
Some species genuinely collect objects:
- **Goblins** favour shiny objects,
- **Orcs** often collect bones,
- **Ratmen** collect technological oddities and combine them into even stranger technological oddities with an appreciable chance of explosion,
- **true dragons** hoard an enormous range of objects/concepts according to draconic hoard psychology.

Other creatures may collect scents, trophies, nesting materials, or magical objects.

## 34.18 Resource depletion
Repeated harvesting really depletes resources.

Mine all the ore, harvest every magical fungus, drain a spring, or eliminate a venom-producing population and there is no invisible reset timer.

Recovery depends on geology, ecology, reproduction, hydrology, magic, and time.

## 34.19 Dungeon ownership and law
Especially after Lock-In, “dungeon clearing” can involve:
- trespass,
- theft,
- grave robbery,
- poaching,
- vandalism,
- unlawful excavation,
- salvage disputes,
- noble/temple/state/Dwarven property claims.

Players cannot assume that “abandoned-looking” means legally ownerless.

## 34.20 Ecological shocks after Lock-In
Pre-Lock hidden balancing systems may have quietly prevented certain player metas from destroying ecosystems.

When those systems disappear at Lock-In, previously sustainable-looking farming can cause:
- predator crashes,
- herbivore booms,
- vegetation decline,
- crop damage,
- disease outbreaks,
- predator migration,
- regional economic disruption.

Players may not initially know which ecological relationships were artificially stabilised before the transition.

---

# 35. III.D.7 — Legendary Beasts, Named Monsters & World Bosses

## 35.1 Legendary status is historical, not biological
“Legendary creature” is a social/historical label.

A member of an ordinary species can become legendary through:
- extreme age or size,
- unusual magic,
- survival of famous battles,
- killing notable people,
- terrorising a region for generations,
- extraordinary bonds,
- accumulated stories and folklore.

Porferion is legendary because he is **Porferion**, not because he reached a biological “Legendary” stage.

## 35.2 “World boss” is player terminology
NPCs use terms such as:
- great beast,
- scourge,
- named monster,
- ancient one,
- old terror,
- regional epithet.

At first, many NPCs find “world boss” confusing. Over prolonged contact, some begin adopting player terminology.

## 35.3 No ontological boss levels
Named creatures do not possess literal visible reality-level numbers.

Pre-Lock developer guidance may estimate difficulty, but “recommended level 40+ group” is a game interface judgment, not a metaphysical fact.

## 35.4 No level scaling
Legendary creatures do **not** scale to attackers.

A new player attacking an Ancient Gorebrooder gets the same Ancient Gorebrooder that an elite raid gets.

Conversely, a once-terrifying creature can become realistically manageable as players improve in skill, equipment, organisation, and knowledge.

## 35.5 Named individuals are unique
There is only one Porferion, one Cannibal, one Red Mother, etc.

Competing guilds are pursuing the same persistent individual.

## 35.6 Respawn hierarchy before Lock-In
Pre-Lock replenishment differs by status:
- **minor bosses** may be replaced naturally or through hidden game population systems;
- **some major bosses** may be replaced by offspring, ecological successors, magical regeneration, or developer intervention;
- **true world bosses do not respawn** merely for repeatable content.

A true world-boss kill is a persistent historical event even before Lock-In unless that creature's established supernatural nature itself includes genuine return.

## 35.7 Post-Lock death permanence
After Lock-In, ordinary death is final.

If Porferion dies:
- Porferion is gone,
- his carcass exists,
- territory changes,
- scavengers arrive,
- materials enter markets,
- political/ecological effects unfold,
- the event becomes history.

There is no weekly reset.

## 35.8 Titles can outlive individuals
A local title may be inherited by successive animals.

Thus records of “the Fen King” across four centuries may describe several different creatures, creating historical confusion.

## 35.9 Multiple names across cultures
A single legendary creature can have different names in different languages/cultures, plus a separate player nickname.

Identifying whether two legends describe the same animal can become serious historical/naturalist research.

## 35.10 Folklore is unreliable
Monster stories can be:
- true,
- exaggerated,
- conflated,
- completely false,
- accidentally made true by magic.

Claims such as “steel cannot kill it” or “it appears every thirteen years” require evidence rather than automatic acceptance.

## 35.11 Survival creates escalation
Repeatedly surviving hunts can make an individual more dangerous through:
- learned tactics,
- scars and physical adaptation,
- magical adaptation,
- recognition of weapon types,
- avoidance behaviour,
- altered hunting patterns,
- accumulated experience.

This creates a natural progression:
**ordinary threat → local named creature → minor boss → major boss → legendary/world-famous boss**.

A minor boss is more likely to become major if it repeatedly survives. A major boss can likewise become legendary.

## 35.12 Persistent injuries
Lost eyes, broken horns, missing limbs, embedded weapons, and badly healed fractures remain.

Injuries may weaken a creature, force altered behaviour, or make it more dangerous through adaptation.

Old Crooked-Leg is an established example.

## 35.13 Kill histories
Notorious creatures can accumulate documented victim lists and oral family histories.

Military archives may list lost patrols. Families may remember specific deaths. Players can become part of those records.

Post-Lock a statistic such as “37 confirmed player deaths” refers to actual permanently dead people.

## 35.14 High-end hunts are expeditions
Truly dangerous named creatures often require:
- scouts,
- guides,
- doctors,
- animal handlers,
- bait,
- siege equipment,
- transport,
- carcass-preservation teams,
- weather planning,
- evacuation routes,
- political permission.

Killing a twelve-ton creature and only afterward asking how to move it is considered poor expedition planning.

## 35.15 Major carcasses create political/economic crises
A legendary carcass can be worth a fortune in:
- blood,
- organs,
- hide,
- bone,
- magical glands,
- scientific specimens,
- religious relics,
- trophies,
- edible material where applicable.

The hardest phase of a hunt may become determining who legally owns the corpse.

## 35.16 Conservation versus extermination
Dangerous named creatures may still be protected because they are:
- rare breeding adults,
- ecologically stabilising,
- sacred,
- scientifically valuable,
- economically/touristically valuable,
- suppressors of worse threats.

Villagers, rangers, temples, governments, hunters, and scholars may therefore want very different outcomes.

## 35.17 Legendary non-hostiles
Not every legendary creature is an aggressor.

Ancient whales, giant herbivores, sacred stags, old magical tortoises, and peaceful leviathans may be harmless unless provoked.

Attacking one because it “looks like a boss” can make an entire region hate the attackers.

## 35.18 Near-person intelligence
Some exceptional beasts reach extraordinary intelligence without necessarily becoming sapient.

They may:
- recognise heraldry,
- identify ambush patterns,
- distinguish civilians from soldiers,
- test people behaviourally,
- manipulate terrain,
- communicate intent indirectly.

Such animals fuel endless arguments over where animal intelligence ends and personhood begins.

## 35.19 Legendary encounter diversity
The canonical legendary roster deliberately includes more than giant predators:
- predators,
- giant herbivores,
- marine leviathans,
- undead,
- aberrations,
- Ancient Gorebrooders,
- giant arthropods,
- territorial magical beasts,
- aerial migrants,
- Frost Giants,
- cursed former people,
- monster colonies,
- environment-altering organisms.

True dragons remain their own category rather than being reduced to ordinary “world bosses.”

## 35.20 First canonical roster of named legendary creatures

### World-famous legends

#### 35.20.1 Porferion, the Black Ruin — Drakoria
Ancient black Breaker Wyvern with catastrophic concussive breath. A true world-boss-scale beast: unique, persistent, unscaled, and permanently killable. Repeated failed hunts make him more experienced.

#### 35.20.2 The Red Mother of Czaivar — northern Choreleth Reach
Ancient Gorebrooder matriarch estimated at over three centuries old. A large share of regional Redfang stock may descend from her. Her blood would be enormously valuable; multiple extermination expeditions have failed.

#### 35.20.3 Cannibal — Drakoria
Huge wild wyvern notorious for killing and eating other wyverns, including juveniles and wounded adults. His presence can disrupt whole breeding territories.

#### 35.20.4 Thyrmarion, the Archipelago Storm — Pearl/Azure/Suralian seas
Highly migratory lightning wyvern following major storm systems. Particularly hated by Suralians for damaged ships, disrupted raids, frightened crews, and at least one alleged roasted-pig theft.

#### 35.20.5 Aouraleth, the Returning Isle — Azure Sea
Extraordinarily ancient Crownback Leviathan. So immense that inexperienced sailors have mistaken her distant back for an island. Peaceful, migratory, and fiercely protective of calves. Her death would cause ecological and political outrage.

#### 35.20.6 The Thirteen-Mouthed Choir — central Vaeloria
A centuries-old **Red Choir supercolony**, not one animal. Its linked organisms operate as an ecological network that progressively transforms surrounding habitat. Destroying one “mouth” redirects activity through the rest.

#### 35.20.7 The Mourning King — western Eldrath
A complete-soul revenant from an ancient war whose original name has been lost beneath folklore. It remains bound to an unfinished military purpose that historians do not fully understand. It can be approached peacefully, but anyone meeting its criteria for “enemy soldier” is in grave danger.

#### 35.20.8 The Hollow Pilgrim — Zakhar
Ancient intelligent Eidophage retaining a disturbing degree of personality despite centuries of soul-feeding. Travels rather than holding territory. Has at times negotiated for access to condemned criminals or the dying instead of openly hunting, prompting intense theological debate.

#### 35.20.9 The Black Orchard — Choreleth Reach
A huge magically altered fungal-organism colony spread across several square kilometres. Animals entering deeper zones can become behaviourally incorporated into its ecology. The environment itself is the encounter.

#### 35.20.10 Urrhakar-of-Nine-Snows — Shimmering Winterlands
A notorious **Frost Giant** and therefore a sapient person, not a monster. Players nevertheless discuss him as a world boss because of his martial reputation. He has destroyed multiple expeditions and regards “hunting him for loot” as proof that smaller peoples deserve consequences.

### Major regional legends

#### 35.20.11 Verathys, the White Dread — Drakorian Enclave
Flightmaster Ysra Vark's immense white frost wyvern, famous for exceptionally potent frost breath and extraordinary coordination with Ysra.

#### 35.20.12 Thermigar — Drakorian Enclave
Warden-Captain Ilyas Krell's fire wyvern. Sustained breath can make stone visibly glow. Less agile than Verathys but exceptionally powerful.

#### 35.20.13 Yeralamyr, Matriarch of the Enclave — Drakoria
Ancient Enclave breeding matriarch and ancestor of many modern wyverns. Exceptionally tolerant and friendly toward Wyvernblooded. Legendary but not a hostile boss unless someone is catastrophically foolish enough to make her one.

#### 35.20.14 Wyrmyriar — Drakoria/Feragia migration route
Large wild frost wyvern migrating seasonally between Drakoria and Feragia. Feragian communities track his annual arrival.

#### 35.20.15 Gyrlaria, the Jungle Tyrant — Drakoria
Colossal acid wyvern inhabiting tropical Drakoria. Trees throughout her territory show enormous chemical scars. Enclave settlement policy toward the area is effectively “do not.”

#### 35.20.16 The Pale Patriarch — beneath Keralfidar
Ancient cavern Gorebrooder known through missing miners, drag marks, shed bristles, and rare sightings. Repeated proposals to capture it for longevity blood founder on the problem of actually restraining it.

#### 35.20.17 Vein-Eater Vash — Choreleth Reach
Oversized Veinseeker Gorebrooder famous for pursuing wounded prey across extreme distances. The story of Vash tracking a wounded soldier for roughly 200 km and later climbing the town wall is substantially grounded in real events.

#### 35.20.18 Old Crooked-Leg — Peromian wetlands
Great Mirebrooder with a badly healed rear-leg injury and distinctive gait. Learned to attack and capsize boats before targeting passengers.

#### 35.20.19 Nine-Roots — Deepwood, Vaeloria
Enormous ancient Rootback with soil, moss, and small plants growing across its shell. Local Elves dispute whether Nine-Roots is a single centuries-old animal or a title inherited by similarly marked Rootbacks.

#### 35.20.20 Ash-Crown — southern Zakhar
Huge magical arthropod predator distantly related to Lantern Scorpions, with mineralised dorsal plates resembling a crown. Its venom is extremely valuable, forcing rangers to protect the animal from poachers while also protecting people from it.

#### 35.20.21 The Widow of Greenmere — Aldemere
Former Human noblewoman afflicted by a badly broken hereditary curse. Spends most of the year as herself but periodically transforms into a large predatory form. Killing the “monster” kills the person, making the case a legal, medical, magical, and ethical nightmare.

#### 35.20.22 Glass-Eater — Cinderl Plains
Large old Glassjaw whose territory includes silica-rich badlands. Has survived enough hunts to recognise traps, ranged formations, and several common magical attack patterns. A textbook example of a minor boss becoming major by surviving players.

### Highly local legends

#### 35.20.23 White-Ear — Gallowood
Very old Gallowood Blacktail buck with one pale ear and a huge asymmetric rack. Multiple generations of local hunters have deliberately spared him, turning him into an unofficial woodland symbol.

#### 35.20.24 Redmouth — Weroxia
Infamous Weroxian Vinehare blamed for decades of damage to prestigious vineyards. Multiple vineyards claim to have killed Redmouth, raising the possibility that “Redmouth” is a title repeatedly inherited by whichever hare is currently destroying the most expensive grapes.

#### 35.20.25 Hookbeak — Suralian Isles
Ancient Suralian Hookbird that follows pirate vessels and steals unattended food, jewellery, and small knives. Multiple crews claim ownership; Hookbeak recognises none of those claims.

#### 35.20.26 Three-Toes — northern Eldrath
Old Barkmantle Cat missing two toes on one forepaw. Famous for approaching some camps peacefully and appearing able to distinguish experienced woodsmen from reckless intruders.

#### 35.20.27 Barreljaw — Zakhar wetlands
Enormous Marsh Bellower bull. Not predatory, merely exceptionally bad-tempered. Has destroyed carts, fences, boats, and one tax office after an ill-advised attempt to drive him through a market.

#### 35.20.28 Silverbelly — near Westmere
An otherwise ordinary wolf made legendary after an early player gutted it and found swallowed silver coins from a previous victim. The forum story helped trigger the temporary player craze for gutting every wolf in case valuables were inside.

#### 35.20.29 Bellbreaker — Stormspine
Exceptionally large Crag Gnawer whose tunnelling caused an abandoned gallery to collapse beneath a Dwarven warning bell. Dwarves insist it is not legendary while maintaining detailed records of its movements for decades.

#### 35.20.30 Saint's Lantern — Fahtarin
Unusually old Lantern Hind with conspicuous golden-white nocturnal markings. Several local religious traditions treat encountering her as auspicious. She is legally protected; killing her is an efficient method for making an entire province furious.

## 35.21 Dragon-specific exclusion
The **twelve true dragons** remain a distinct class of sapient ancient beings and are not flattened into ordinary world-boss taxonomy.

A player may colloquially call a hostile true dragon a boss; that does not make the dragon ecologically or metaphysically equivalent to a monster encounter.

## 35.22 Intentional unresolved meta possibility — Vorthraktalyrionth and system awareness
A deliberately unresolved hidden-lore possibility exists around **Vorthraktalyrionth/the Devourer**.

Developers may assign extremely dramatic encounter music when players provoke him. The running example is the developers deciding to play **“Vordt of the Boreal Valley”** during an especially disastrous confrontation.

Vorthraktalyrionth may visibly notice or resent the presentation layer, potentially addressing the developers/music as though he can perceive it.

This is **intentionally unresolved**, not an accidental Phase-VI omission. It must not be generalised into “all true dragons can perceive the system.” If a campaign ever reaches the relevant evidence, the simulator may causally resolve whether Vorthraktalyrionth possesses unusually deep metaphysical perception, can sense particular presentation-layer interventions, or whether another explanation fits the established state.

Until then, preserve the uncertainty. Tomas Bell remains the only explicitly established narrator-aware mortal.

---

# 36. Phase III.E — Institutions, Orders & Major Organisations

## 36.1 General institutional rules
Major institutions persist as organisations in their own right. They possess property, archives, legal privileges, debts, reputations, objectives, procedures, enemies, traditions, and internal cultures that outlive individual leaders.

Institutional competence is multidimensional rather than a single quality score. One organisation may be excellent at archives and finance but poor at field investigation; another may be militarily superb and administratively chaotic.

Advanced legal systems recognise many institutions as legal persons able to own land, contract, borrow, sue and be sued, employ soldiers, inherit property, and receive charters. Foreign recognition varies by jurisdiction.

Transnational organisations are not automatically supranational. Each branch must negotiate with the law and politics of the state in which it operates. **Ferrum Argentarium** and the **Sailing League** are the two largest transnational organisations in Aetheria.

Large organisations naturally develop factions, caucuses, schools of thought, and bureaucratic rivalries. **Ferrum Argentarium is an unusual exception in being strikingly uniform by Aetherian standards**, owing to intense standardisation, audit culture, professional training, and internal discipline.

Leadership succession can be elective, appointed, seniority-based, hereditary, examination-based, religious, rotational, or otherwise culture-specific. No universal “guildmaster” model exists.

Archives are political assets. Control of records can determine land title, genealogy, contracts, criminal evidence, magical knowledge, diplomatic commitments, and historical legitimacy. Destroying or altering archives can change history materially.

## 36.2 Religious orders
The Threefold religious world is decentralised. The High Tripret is enormously important in Eldrath and adjacent spheres but is not a universal pope-equivalent. Other cultures can recognise, reinterpret, or simply ignore Eldrathi ecclesiastical authority.

Specialised religious orders commonly serve all three gods while emphasising particular functions.

### Order of the Closed Mouth
Existing funerary order whose members take a vow of silence. Its doctrine holds that the dead no longer speak and the living must not put words into their mouths. Members prepare bodies, supervise burials, identify the dead, maintain mortuary records, and assist at mass-fatality sites.

### Order of Open Hands
Elania-associated healers, hospice keepers, midwives, famine-relief organisers, and carers for the chronically ill. They operate hospices in several states and are famous for treating enemy wounded during wars when permitted.

### Order of Stone Measure
Garlaer-associated engineers, surveyors, masons, bridge-builders, disaster-recovery specialists, and custodians of structurally dangerous sacred sites. Their maxim is that sound construction is a form of reverence.

### Order of the Last Lantern
Marzoth-associated battlefield mortuary clergy and disaster-death specialists. They recover bodies, prevent corpse desecration, contain dangerous post-mortem magic, and help distinguish legitimate funerary practice from forbidden soul-binding.

### Order of Three Witnesses
Threefold archivists, oath-recorders, genealogists, and custodians of important ritual/legal records. Their copies of treaties, marriages, successions, and religious judgments are often accepted as high-quality secondary evidence.

## 36.3 Militant religious orders
These are not one generic paladin caste. Each has a narrow mission, legal status, theology, and political history.

### Dawn Paladins
Protect Elanian shrines, pilgrims, hospices, sacred groves, and vulnerable civilians during religious travel. Strongly associated with defensive warfare and rescue.

### Ashen Paladins
Marzothian specialists in dangerous undeath, corpse desecration, rogue necromancy, and soul-crime containment. Their reputation is grim but not inherently sinister; they are often the people summoned when a battlefield refuses to stay dead.

### Wardstone Paladins
Garlaerian militant engineers and guardians. They protect major bridges, mountain passes, sacred geological sites, dams, fortified shrines, and magical containment structures, and are trained to fight while maintaining infrastructure under attack.

## 36.4 Magic regulation and arcane institutions
### Concord of Arcane Practice
Headquartered in Fahtarin and led by Archmagister **Célaine Vaussard**, 67. It is a non-sovereign international standards and ethics body, influential but legally dependent on host states. Internal blocs include cautious traditionalists, experimentalists, soul-healing specialists, and decentralists.

### Office of Final Boundaries
Fahtarin forbidden-magic authority under Master Examiner **Dorvain Mérisse**, 56. Investigates prohibited soul manipulation, forced resurrection, reality/time violations, and related crimes.

### Black Lantern Commission
Aldemerian forbidden-magic investigative body under **Lady Hereward Tholyn**, 41. Particularly strong at legal process, intelligence coordination, archival investigation, and cross-crown jurisdiction.

### Ash Wing
Epsilaphtocratorian magical-security apparatus under **Talvarik Shaevorn**, 45. Highly capable, heavily politicised, and closely tied to regime security.

## 36.5 The Wisteria League
The **Wisteria League** is a colloquial rather than fully centralised legal league: a cluster of Vaeloria’s most prestigious universities whose campuses maintain old traditions of planting wisteria at foundation, re-charter, graduation, and major scholarly commemorations. The oldest vines are institutional symbols and rival schools argue over whose are oldest, largest, or descended from the most prestigious cuttings.

Major Wisteria-League schools:

### Estermorion National University
Estermorion. Broadest civic university in the group; especially prestigious in law, medicine, political economy, natural philosophy, public administration, and constitutional scholarship. Its faculty culture is argumentative and unusually tolerant of public disagreement.

### College of the Seven Boughs
Ylav’ralra. Ancient Elven institution strongest in history, philology, memory studies, high magic, diplomacy, and long-duration archival scholarship. Aristocratic patronage remains influential without wholly controlling admissions.

### Royal Academy of Shapdux
Shapdux. Renowned for archaeology, desert ecology, medicine, law, astronomy, hydraulic engineering, and historical linguistics. Its court connections are substantial but the Academy has repeatedly defended scholarly autonomy from regents and ministers.

### Feragian Collegium of Winter and Hearth
Feragia. Exceptional in cold-weather medicine, logistics, military studies, ecology, architecture, food preservation, and survival sciences. Winterguard officers frequently study there without the Collegium being subordinate to the Winterguard.

### Tukrkryur Institute of Stone and Measure
Krklrarkti. Prestigious in engineering, materials, civil construction, anti-magic architecture, surveying, and Lizardish legal-administrative studies. Its magical scholarship focuses more on resistance, containment, and measurement than free magical practice.

### Rukt’fordur Academy of Deep Letters
Chof’krol’kurdr. Dark-Elven prestige school famous for epigraphy, archaeology, subterranean history, comparative languages, statecraft, mineralogy, and difficult archival reconstruction. Foreign students often find its social and political culture intimidating.

### Arkonoranou Aeric Institute
Epsilaphtocratoria. Genuinely prestigious in aerodynamics, Averi physiology, mathematics, engineering, and high-altitude studies, while also being heavily censored and ideologically shaped by the supremacist state. Its research quality and its political environment are both real facts.

## 36.6 Medicine and professional certification
Major cities can sustain hospitals, hospices, quarantine facilities, surgical wards, birthing houses, and magical-treatment centres. Magic supplements rather than replaces anatomy, surgery, sanitation, pharmacology, nursing, epidemiology, and long-term care.

### Collegium of Physickers
A transnational professional medical association that publishes treatment standards, recognises training routes, accredits some hospitals, and mediates disputes over medical competence. Its certifications are respected but not legally mandatory everywhere.

### Circle of the Mended Thread
Professional body for **soul-healers**. Maintains one of the strictest certification systems in Aetheria because an incompetent soul-healer can damage identity as well as health. Particularly important to long-lived undead patients suffering anchoring degradation.

Licensing regimes vary by state and can include physicians, soul-healers, alchemists, magical practitioners, engineers, navigators, monster handlers, lawyers, and assayers.

## 36.7 Economic and transport institutions
### Ferrum Argentarium
Independent under House **Ferrus**, led by Director **Octavian Ferrus**, 64. One of the two largest transnational organisations in Aetheria and the dominant international financial institution.

Ferrum is unusually competent across accounting, auditing, security, logistics, negotiation, legal drafting, intelligence, cross-border finance, debt restructuring, coin handling, and institutional continuity. It is less interested in broad public education, although its internal schools are elite in accounting, finance, law, logistics, and military/security training.

Its internal culture is unusually uniform. Standard procedures, layered audits, cross-checking, professional examinations, and deliberate institutional socialisation reduce the factionalism common elsewhere. House Ferrus has enormous prestige within the institution, but succession to the Directorship is not automatically hereditary; **Lucen Ferrus**, 36, is only presumptive.

Ferrum issues **iron Argents**, operates branches and partner relationships, maintains reserves, clears accounts, issues letters of credit, finances states and wars, and can move value internationally without moving equivalent masses of coin.

### Sailing League
Azure-rooted but transnational, and the other largest cross-border institution. It coordinates pilots, charts, convoy rules, maritime rescue obligations, port arbitration, vessel standards, navigational training, and inter-island commercial norms. It is politically independent of the rotating Azure High Chieftain despite close cooperation.

### Pearl Factors’ Exchange
Based in **Kaolarya**. Major commodity exchange, warehousing, insurance, shipping-contract, and arbitration institution serving the Pearl Archipelago and foreign merchants. It competes and cooperates with Ferrum: Ferrum dominates finance; the Exchange dominates many physical-goods markets.

### Whitewing Relay
A Vaelorian courier network with strong Averi participation and extensive ground/sea partnerships. Known for time-sensitive diplomatic, commercial, medical, and legal dispatches. It cannot make distance disappear; weather, war, migration, and border closures still matter.

## 36.8 Military and security organisations
### Winterguard
Feragia’s famous sworn defensive order. It remains a state military institution with its established oath, permanent service culture, anti-Winterlands mission, and strong institutional identity beyond any single commander.

### Drakorian Enclave
Exclusively Wyvernblooded persistent mortal guild/settlement network in Drakoria, with rider schools, hatcheries, veterinarians, bloodline records, mobile settlements, aerial doctrine, and centuries of continuity.

### Consecrated Lances
Fahtarin’s prestigious knightly formation under Captain-General **Sir Gharévan Vaudrec**, 49. Deeply intertwined with Threefold ceremony but still a military order rather than universal church police.

### Nine Undying
Keralfidar’s famous Gorebrooder-blood-dependent elite warriors. They function as both military unit and state symbol. Their longevity makes them valuable institutional memory but also leaves them dependent on a continuing blood supply.

### Seven Banners Company
Vaelorian mixed-species mercenary company famous for disciplined combined-arms work, clear contracts, and refusing jobs that require extermination of civilians. Its reputation lets it charge more than larger but less trustworthy companies.

### Brass Jackals Company
Zakhar-based mercenary organisation specialising in desert campaigning, caravan warfare, monster-heavy terrain, and siege escort. They are hard, expensive, and pragmatic; their central institutional virtue is that once a contract is formally sealed they are unusually reliable about honouring its written terms.

## 36.9 Adventuring, hunting, exploration, and ranger institutions
Aetheria has **no universal Adventurers’ Guild**. Players use that phrase for a patchwork of employment halls, hunter associations, bounty offices, mercenary registries, exploration societies, tavern boards, and civic contract offices. NPCs may initially be confused when asked for “the Adventurers’ Guild.”

### Iron Trackers’ Compact
Professional monster-hunter association. Maintains kill records, field guides, specialist referrals, salvage norms, and contract arbitration. Its senior members care as much about preparation and ecology as fighting skill.

### Lantern Path Society
Exploration and cartographic society supporting expeditions, survey work, natural history, route finding, and remote-field research. **Captain Gerald Cook** is a recurring celebrity around its circles without being reducible to an employee of the Society.

### Delvers’ Collegium
Archaeological and ruin-exploration association. Tries to distinguish research from looting, negotiates excavation rights, trains specialists in dangerous sites, and frequently clashes with treasure hunters, states, temples, and descendant communities.

### Greenward Rangers
Eldrathian ranger tradition with regionally chartered branches. Handles anti-poaching, forest/fire management, population surveys, dangerous-animal response, breeding-season protection, trail maintenance, and investigation of illegal wildlife trade.

### Whitebough Rangers
Feragian and northern-Vaelorian ranger organisation focused on cold-region ecology, migration monitoring, frost-wyvern routes, monster displacement, remote rescue, and Winterlands boundary management.

### Azure Reefwardens
Marine rangers operating through cooperative agreements among Azure communities. Protect breeding grounds, monitor leviathan routes, police destructive fishing and poaching, escort researchers, and manage dangerous marine-animal encounters.

## 36.10 Resistance organisations
### Road Without Chains
Zhaburan anti-slavery resistance. Contains competing extraction, armed-revolt, reformist, and revolutionary tendencies rather than one ideology.

### Buried Dawn
Resistance network in Gor’oxuer’funro opposing Dark-Elven rule, slavery, experimentation, and undead militarisation.

### Second Sky
Epsilaphtocratorian resistance metaphor/network including non-Averi and Averi dissidents. Cellular structure reflects the regime’s surveillance capacity.

### Blank Pages
Jelorbiduran dissident network opposing the hereditary surveillance dictatorship. Partially infiltrated, meaning membership and information are dangerous even within the movement.

## 36.11 Criminal and clandestine organisations
### Black Keel Syndicate
Maritime smuggling network spanning Pearl and Suralian routes. Moves contraband, fugitives, stolen cargo, illicit monster products, and occasionally people. It is a network of captains, factors, dock officials, and fences rather than one pirate fleet.

### Hollow Market
Distributed fencing and illicit-antiquities network. There is no single physical “Hollow Market”; the name describes trusted brokers, coded auctions, back-room dealers, ruin-looters, and buyers spread across multiple cities.

### Black Ledger Circle
Financial-crime network specialising in forged letters of credit, counterfeiting, fraudulent manifests, laundering, shell partnerships, and insider theft. Ferrum Argentarium regards it as a persistent institutional enemy.

### Quiet Knives
Assassination-broker network rather than one school of killers. It matches clients with specialist assassins, arranges deniable payment, and tries to keep clients and contractors separated. Famous independent assassins such as the **Pale Bell** need not belong to it.

## 36.12 Institutional consequences after Lock-In
After Lock-In, institutions become even more important because there is no game abstraction to replace them. Archives, licensing, hospitals, banking, courier networks, ranger patrols, universities, contract law, logistics, and professional organisations become survival infrastructure.

Player attempts to introduce Earth systems are treated as proposals, not cheat codes. Existing organisations have incentives, traditions, competent officials, vested interests, and local knowledge. Some adopt player ideas quickly; others resist; many hybridise them.

The strongest organisations can survive incompetent leaders for a time because their procedures and personnel persist. Conversely, a brilliant leader cannot instantly repair a rotten institution.

# 37. Phase III.F — Player Population & Demographics

## 37.1 Scale of Aetheria Online
Aetheria Online is one of the largest entertainment products in human history.
- **Initial launch accounts:** approximately **100 million** across the first-wave territories.
- **Registered accounts by the first anniversary:** approximately **2 billion**.
- **Typical monthly active players immediately before the anniversary event:** approximately **750 million**.
- **Players online at the exact Lock-In moment:** approximately **875 million**.
- **Total players trapped after roughly two weeks of continued logins:** approximately **1.65 billion**.

The anniversary surge is exceptional: the 875 million simultaneous Lock-In cohort includes many returning/lapsed players who were not part of the prior ordinary 30-day active population, so the event itself immediately pushes the rolling active-user count above the pre-anniversary 750 million baseline.

The initial Lock-In cohort equals **43.75% of all registered accounts**. By the two-week crisis point, approximately **82.5% of registered accounts** have entered Aetheria and become trapped. About **775 million additional people** enter after the original Lock-In because outsiders do not immediately understand what has happened. The exact mechanism, public discovery timeline, and reason for the Lock-In timing belong to later First-Year/Real-World/Hidden-Lore phases.

## 37.2 Rollout order
Aetheria Online launches in waves rather than globally on day one.

**First wave — global Launch Day:**
- United States
- Canada
- Australia
- United Kingdom
- New Zealand
- Ireland
- Japan
- South Korea
- China
- Thailand
- Vietnam

**Second wave:**
- wider Europe
- Mexico

**Third wave:**
- remainder of Asia, including the Middle East and Central Asia where local approval is obtained
- remainder of Oceania and Pacific jurisdictions

**Fourth wave:**
- South America
- Central America and the Caribbean generally enter during the same wider Latin American expansion, with Mexico already live from Wave Two

**Fifth wave:**
- Africa

### Standard-campaign availability anchors
The exact day on which every individual jurisdiction opens need not be fixed unless a campaign requires it, but the three standard campaign starts use these anchors:

- **Launch Day:** official access is available only in the **First Wave** countries listed above.
- **Six Months After Launch:** **Waves One through Three** are officially live. The wider Latin American/Caribbean and African rollouts have not yet become ordinary standard access.
- **First Anniversary:** **all five waves** have officially launched in participating jurisdictions, although local regulation, hardware scarcity, licensing, pricing and service coverage still vary.

A player may originate from an unsupported country before its official wave only through a deliberately **non-standard** route such as grey-market imported hardware, travel to a supported jurisdiction, institutional access, or other plausible exception. The simulator must not silently pretend that a country had ordinary official access before its rollout.

The selected Earth starting country is the character's **country/territory of residence or physical access at the beginning of the campaign**, not necessarily their citizenship or ethnicity. Those may differ and should not be inferred automatically.

The staggered rollout produces meaningful differences in average experience, established guilds, wealth, map knowledge, and high-level progression by the anniversary. First-wave populations contain a disproportionate share of veterans. A character joining at the global six-month or anniversary date may therefore live in a country where Aetheria has been officially available for less than the full global age of the game.

## 37.3 Lock-In timing and demographic skew
VRSAGIM deliberately chooses the **first anniversary** because it knows concurrency will be unprecedented and wants to extend its perceived “second chance” to as many people as possible. Korigan, not VRSAGIM, is responsible for most anniversary programming and publicity, although VRSAGIM subtly supports rather than suppresses high participation. The exact clock time can still be globally inconvenient and produces substantial time-zone skew.

Consequences:
- the initial **875 million** are demographically and geographically skewed by time zone, work/school schedules, the anniversary celebration, and first-wave adoption;
- exact country-by-country proportions of the first instant remain intentionally approximate unless a campaign requires a fixed clock time;
- continued logins over the next two weeks substantially smooth the skew, making the eventual **1.65 billion** trapped population much more globally mixed.

## 37.4 Broad final regional composition
Canonical approximate composition of the roughly 1.65 billion trapped population after two weeks:

| Region | Share | Approx. trapped players |
|---|---:|---:|
| East Asia | 27% | 445.5 million |
| South & Southeast Asia | 20% | 330.0 million |
| Europe | 17% | 280.5 million |
| North America | 15% | 247.5 million |
| Latin America & Caribbean | 9% | 148.5 million |
| Africa | 7% | 115.5 million |
| Middle East & Central Asia | 3.5% | 57.75 million |
| Oceania | 1.5% | 24.75 million |

Australia is unusually prominent for its population because it was a first-wave market and full-dive adoption is very high. A canonical approximate share of roughly **1.1% of the final trapped population** gives approximately **18.15 million Australians**, with the remainder of the Oceania total primarily New Zealand and Pacific-region players. These are setting-scale demographic estimates rather than an exact 2057 census.

## 37.5 Minimum age and age distribution
Official full-dive access is **16+**. The biometric/neural calibration system can detect age well enough that ordinary under-16 use simply fails rather than relying only on a checkbox. Circumvention is possible but technically extreme; a sufficiently obsessive child hacker can, in principle, spend months defeating it.

Canonical approximate trapped age distribution:

| Age | Share | Approx. players |
|---|---:|---:|
| Under 16 via circumvention | 0.02% | 0.33 million |
| 16–17 | 3.98% | 65.67 million |
| 18–24 | 22% | 363.0 million |
| 25–34 | 24% | 396.0 million |
| 35–49 | 16% | 264.0 million |
| 50–64 | 11% | 181.5 million |
| 65–79 | 17% | 280.5 million |
| 80+ | 6% | 99.0 million |

Aetheria has an unusual **double demographic peak**: young adults and older/elderly users. Young adults dominate conventional gaming culture, while full-dive VR is exceptionally attractive to older people and many people with physical disabilities because avatar control can bypass many peripheral physical limitations and restore forms of movement or embodied activity unavailable to them on Earth.

## 37.6 Gender balance
The playerbase is comparatively balanced, with only a mild male skew. Canonical approximate distribution by self-described gender is:
- **51% men**
- **47% women**
- **2% nonbinary / other descriptions**

This should not be confused with initial avatar-body sex. Initial character creation is biometrically constrained as described in Phase II: players cannot simply select any sex/body at the creation screen. In-world modification later allows much greater bodily change.

## 37.7 Character bodies and biometric grounding
At initial creation:
- biological sex matches the player's real-world biological sex;
- appearance/body remains recognisably derived from the player's scan;
- chosen species reshapes that baseline according to real Aetherian anatomy;
- full-dive calibration establishes proportions, reflexes, sensory baselines, and physical control.

Players therefore do not begin by freely designing arbitrary 205-cm idealised bodies, radically different ages, or unrelated faces. Later in-game magic, alchemy, technology, surgery, and other embodied systems can produce extensive changes, including sex/species alteration where feasible.

At Lock-In, players already possess basic proprioceptive competence in their current species body because full-dive play has required embodied control all year. They do not suddenly forget how to walk. However, subtle species-specific social/physical mastery remains learned skill: tail language, ear positioning, scent interpretation, wing handling, non-Human posture, unusual senses, and other anatomy can take substantial expertise.

## 37.8 Playable-species distribution
Base playable-species distribution among the final trapped population is approximately:

| Species | Share | Approx. players |
|---|---:|---:|
| Human | 42% | 693.0 million |
| Elf | 15% | 247.5 million |
| Dark Elf | 9% | 148.5 million |
| Nymph | 6% | 99.0 million |
| Golem | 4% | 66.0 million |
| Automaton | 4% | 66.0 million |
| Gatan | 5% | 82.5 million |
| Lupiveni | 4.5% | 74.25 million |
| Lizardman | 4% | 66.0 million |
| Satyr | 3.5% | 57.75 million |
| Averi | 3% | 49.5 million |

Beastfolk collectively account for **20%**. Humans are the largest single choice but far from a majority.

**Wyvernblooded is not counted as a separate base species in this table.** It is the previously established rare cross-cutting lineage/trait received by approximately **1% of completed player characters**, meaning on the order of **16.5 million** of the eventual trapped cohort may possess it if the one-percent rate remains globally stable. Genuine wyvern bonding remains vastly rarer because compatible animals, soul compatibility, training, geography, and survival all constrain it. **Dragonblooded** remains ultra-rare: only a couple of random launch-era players are intended to possess it.

## 37.9 Cultural patterns in species choice
Species selection varies substantially across countries, age groups, fandoms, and subcultures without being deterministic.
- Humans lead almost everywhere through familiarity and flexible fantasy identity.
- Elves and Dark Elves are unusually popular in several East Asian markets and among fantasy/Warhammer-adjacent fandoms.
- Beastfolk draw players from furry communities, nature/mythology enthusiasts, and many regional fandoms; some African markets show unusually strong Beastfolk uptake as a contingent cultural/marketing trend rather than an inherent demographic trait.
- Nymphs are notably overrepresented in Oceania and among players strongly attracted to aquatic/coastal play.
- Golems and Automatons develop strong engineering, roleplay, construct-fantasy, and technology-oriented subcultures.

## 37.10 Translation and Earth-language mixing
Pre-Lock, the game engine performs enormous amounts of invisible localisation/translation work.
- Common spoken by an English-language player may be rendered as English to that player.
- The same Common sentence can be rendered as Japanese to a Japanese-language player.
- Both players are still treated as speaking **Common** to one another; Earth-language settings do not prevent conversation.
- Unknown Aetherian languages remain meaningfully unknown according to the established language-learning system.

At Lock-In, the convenience/localisation layer disappears as previously established. Players encounter actual Aetherian speech directly, producing a major linguistic and educational shock that later player culture must solve.

## 37.11 Hardcore versus casual population
Aetheria's extraordinary simulation depth attracts far more than combat optimisers.

Canonical approximate play-style intensity:
- **7% hardcore/competitive/high-end progression** — ~115.5 million
- **23% regular/core players** — ~379.5 million
- **70% casual or semi-casual players** — ~1.155 billion

“Casual” does not mean useless. Many of these players are accomplished crafters, farmers, doctors, merchants, builders, musicians, animal handlers, explorers, navigators, scholars, roleplayers, or social organisers.

## 37.12 Primary play identities
Categories overlap in reality, but an approximate primary-activity snapshot before Lock-In is:
- combat/adventure — 33%
- exploration/travel — 14%
- crafting/industry — 13%
- social/roleplay/arts — 10%
- commerce/administration — 8%
- scholarship/archaeology/lore — 6%
- construction/engineering — 6%
- agriculture/animal handling — 5%
- medicine/support/healing — 5%

The huge non-combat population becomes one of the strongest reasons post-Lock player civilisation does not immediately collapse.

## 37.13 Guild membership
At initial launch, **zero players begin in guilds**. Guild participation is unlocked only after the tutorial/system introduction, and every guild must therefore be built socially after launch.

By the first anniversary, guild membership is roughly **50/50**: about half of active players belong to a guild and half remain unguilded or operate through informal friend groups.

Post-Lock, the long-term tendency is toward roughly **90% of surviving players** belonging to a guild or guild-like mutual-support organisation. The pace, causes, coercion, politics, and exact timing of that transformation belong to Phase III.J.

## 37.14 Demographic consequences
The 1.65-billion-person Lock-In population is not a neat sample of humanity. It is shaped by:
- rollout order;
- hardware affordability/access;
- regional gaming culture;
- full-dive accessibility;
- anniversary participation;
- the exact Lock-In time;
- the two-week period in which people continue logging in;
- age restrictions and circumvention;
- one-character biometric embodiment;
- established player communities and guilds.

Initial post-Lock society therefore begins with strong regional/time-zone distortions, but the continuing influx over two weeks makes the final trapped population far more globally mixed than the original 875-million cohort.

---


# 38. Phase III.G — Famous Players, Guilds & Player Organisations

## 38.1 Player names and NPC adaptation
Every player has a globally unique **character/account name** plus a real-world identity that is not automatically public to other players.

Aetherian NPCs do not necessarily reproduce awkward usernames literally. They naturally interpret, shorten, translate, or phonologically reshape them according to local language and naming habits. Thus **IronBishop** might become something like **Ferris Bishop** in one Common-speaking region, a more literal iron-related form elsewhere, or even a loosely associated ordinary personal name such as **Ethan Bishop** where that is socially easier. Different cultures can independently settle on different Aetherian names for the same player.

Names already resembling plausible names are often spoken unchanged: **Aragorn**, **TywinLannister**, **TonyStark**, etc. Numeric suffixes create problems. An NPC who encounters **TywinLannister1** may simply hear/say “Tywin Lannister”; if multiple Tywins are known, NPCs distinguish them by epithets, origin, guild, chronology, or reputation — e.g. **Tywin the Clever**, **Tywin the Foolish**, **the First Tywin**, **Tywin of Westmere**. Obscene or unpronounceable names may be softened into usable local names.

Post-Lock, system username overlays disappear. Earth names, usernames, and locally acquired Aetherian names can coexist, and different communities may know the same person by different names.

## 38.2 Fame and public reputation
Player fame is fragmented by community. Raiders, traders, roleplayers, crafters, hunters, scholars, explorers, streamers, speedrunners, and PvP players maintain partially separate celebrity cultures. Only a tiny number become globally recognisable.

Pre-Lock Aetheria has enormous streaming/video culture. Fame can come from excellence, teaching, humour, storytelling, chaos, scandals, exploration, spectacular failure, or pure personality. Post-Lock, old creator fame may provide social influence but guarantees neither competence nor political legitimacy.

Official rankings are category-specific: tournaments, races, first clears, exploration records, crafting competitions, PvP ladders, monster kills, and similar metrics. There is **no objective overall #1 player**.

Community reputation is fallible. Famous fighters may benefit from favourable matchmaking, edited footage, exceptional support teams, equipment, or selective risk-taking; obscure players may be far more capable than their visibility suggests.

## 38.3 Guild mechanics — restored prior canon
Formal guild creation requires a small founding group (normally at least five players), a unique registered guild name, a charter/governance declaration, a registration fee, and a designated contact seat or recognised point of administration. A physical headquarters is not initially mandatory.

There is no practical low hard cap on guild membership. Guilds range from tiny friend groups to pre-Lock megaguilds numbering in the millions. Management complexity is real rather than solved by magic UI.

Existing command-scale canon:
- **Party:** 2–10
- **Raid:** 11–40
- **Warband:** 41–150
- **Host:** 151–1,000
- **Army:** 1,001–10,000
- **Grand Host:** 10,000+

These describe operating formations, not guild membership caps; megaguilds subdivide into many such formations.

Guild governance is fully player-defined within system/legal constraints: founder rule, democracy, councils, corporate boards, military hierarchy, cooperatives, rotating offices, roleplay monarchies, anarchic collectives, etc.

Guilds may collectively own property, money, ships, farms, workshops, warehouses, equipment, animals, fortifications, armies, and territory where local NPC law recognises that ownership. Territorial control comes through purchase, lease, charter, treaty, conquest followed by recognition, or other real political processes — never capture circles. Large guilds can develop dues, salaries, pensions, equipment lending, internal credit, ration systems, and other institutional economies.

Before Lock-In, roughly half the population belongs to a guild. Post-Lock the long-run tendency is around 90% affiliation with guilds or guild-like mutual-support organisations.

## 38.4 Geographic and corporate patterns
Common auto-translation weakens real-world language barriers, so most guilds are substantially more international and multiethnic than conventional MMO guilds. National/regional guilds still exist, including a small number tied to real-world organised-crime traditions, but they are not the default social form.

Real-world companies, universities, esports organisations, entertainment brands, and other institutions sponsor player organisations before Lock-In. **Creative Assembly** is the first major entertainment company to sponsor a prominent Aetheria crossover organisation, the **Grand Warhost**, built around strategy events, mass-battle competitions, historical/fantasy military roleplay, and tactical education. After Lock-In, corporate ownership of in-world assets becomes legally and philosophically absurd: the Earth-side sponsor cannot practically administer the property, while the trapped members physically live there.

## 38.5 Major pre-Lock guilds
| Guild | Approx. anniversary membership | Identity |
|---|---:|---|
| **Meridian Compact** | 3.4 million | Largest broadly international generalist megaguild; federal structure, trade, defence, exploration, settlement support. |
| **Wayfarer's Rest** | 2.6 million | Casual/social/travel megaguild; inns, guides, community events, newbie support. |
| **Hearthbound** | 1.9 million | Hardcore roleplay/settlement guild with written constitutions, councils, agriculture schedules and medieval civic administration; unexpectedly well prepared for Lock-In. |
| **Atlas Union** | 1.4 million | Exploration, surveying, mapmaking, route documentation and dangerous-region expeditions. |
| **Seventh Forge** | 950,000 | Crafting, industrial production, metallurgy, enchantment and workshop management. |
| **Green Apron Cooperative** | 820,000 | Agriculture, animal breeding, cooking, brewing, food logistics and rural settlement play. |
| **Bastion Twelve** | 690,000 | High-end combat, raid planning and defensive warfare; famous for disciplined combined-arms doctrine. |
| **Common Banner** | 610,000 | Multilingual/multiethnic teaching guild focused on mentoring newer players and cross-region coordination. |
| **The Night Clinic** | 330,000 | Healers, doctors, alchemists and support specialists; includes many real medical professionals and students. |
| **Wisteria Exchange** | 290,000 | Player scholars, university students, historians, linguists and lore researchers with close ties to Vaelorian academic life. |
| **Iron Choir** | 260,000 | Golem/Automaton-heavy engineering, architecture, logistics and construct culture, though membership is open. |
| **Palace of Masks** | 240,000 | Roleplay, diplomacy, etiquette, performance, intrigue and court simulation. |
| **Grand Warhost** | 210,000 | Creative Assembly-sponsored strategy/crossover guild; mass-battle competitions and tactical roleplay. |
| **Skybound Covenant** | 185,000 | Wyvernblooded-heavy network focused on aerial ecology, Enclave contact, riding theory and rare-lineage research. |
| **Free Company of Nine Roads** | 170,000 | Escort, mercenary, caravan-security and contract warfare guild. |
| **Deepdelvers** | 155,000 | Mining, archaeology, caves, ruins and subterranean exploration. |
| **Red Thread** | 120,000 | PvP and small-unit tactical specialists; disproportionately influential despite smaller size. |
| **First Light Rescue** | 105,000 | Search-and-rescue, wilderness recovery, evacuation, disaster response and guide work. |

Only a handful of pre-Lock guilds cross one million members; cohesion at that scale is difficult even with game tools.

## 38.6 Notorious/criminal player organisations
- **Ghost Ledger** — financial fraud, exploit-laundering, forged credit instruments and economic manipulation.
- **Velvet Knife** — pre-Lock assassination-contract/PvP broker network; post-Lock members split sharply over whether to continue once death is permanent.
- **Red Market** — stolen goods, restricted items, illicit monster materials and contraband trading.
- **Empty Throne** — griefing, protection rackets, territorial extortion and coerced access to player-held facilities.
- **Ninth Door** — exploit hunters, forbidden-zone trespassers and illicit magical research; some members treat restrictions as challenges rather than warnings.
- **Kurogane-kai** — relatively small Japanese organised-crime-linked guild with real-world yakuza associations; highly insular compared with normal Aetherian guild culture.
- **La Cadena Roja** — Mexico-based criminal-network guild with cartel-linked membership; involved in smuggling and extortion rather than ordinary roleplay criminality.
- **Famiglia Nera** — small Europe-based mafia-linked network used for laundering, brokerage and private enforcement.

These groups are exceptional, not representative of national player communities.

## 38.7 Important non-guild player organisations
- **Player Medical Consortium** — real doctors, nurses, paramedics, pharmacists, students and healers sharing evidence, treatment protocols and clinical observations.
- **Artisan Exchange Registry** — cross-guild catalogue of reputable player smiths, tailors, engineers, enchanters and manufacturers.
- **Open Hearth Network** — recipes, food-safety knowledge, communal kitchens, famine response and bulk provisioning.
- **Common Tongue Society** — linguists and language hobbyists studying actual Aetherian languages beneath the pre-Lock localisation layer.
- **Earthsong Collective** — musicians, actors, storytellers and artists adapting Earth culture to Aetherian instruments and aesthetics.
- **Player Cartographers' Cooperative** — map standards, survey techniques, route verification and shared geographic records.
- **Accessible Worlds Union** — accessibility-focused community heavily represented by disabled and elderly players; post-Lock becomes important for adapting support norms to new bodies.
- **Living Archive Project** — volunteer historians documenting player events, guild decisions, forum culture and early Aetherian discoveries before records disappear.

## 38.8 Famous players
Fame below is niche-dependent, not an overall ranking. “Post-Lock role” describes an early trajectory, not destiny or plot armour.

| Username | Real identity / origin | Species | Pre-Lock reputation | Early post-Lock niche |

| **BronnOfTheBlackwater** | Callum Reid, Australia | Human | notorious opportunistic PvP duellist/mercenary; reputation greater than his willingness to take fair fights | contract captain and independent fixer rather than conventional hero. |

| **VelvetHammer** | Zofia Kowalska, Poland | Golem | elite smith and materials tester | industrial workshop leader and standards advocate. |
| **ArcMinute** | Nguyễn Minh Châu, Vietnam | Averi | navigator, surveyor and speed-cartographer | air/sea route planning and mapping. |
| **MossMother** | Élodie Marchand, France | Satyr | farmer, breeder, seed collector | agricultural resilience, seed banks and breeding programmes. |
