| **NullMoth** | Rafael Souza, Brazil | Dark Elf | stealth speedrunner and route-breaker | reconnaissance, infiltration and dangerous courier work. |
| **CloudShepherd** | Maia Raukawa, New Zealand | Averi, Wyvernblooded | aerial explorer and rare-lineage researcher | Enclave contact, aerial scouting and wyvern-bond scholarship. |
| **KestrelByte** | Seo Yuna, South Korea | Automaton, Wyvernblooded | animal-behaviour/data specialist fascinated by wyvern cognition | technical liaison between player researchers and Enclave handlers. |
| **RedQuill** | Diego Salazar, Mexico | Dark Elf | merchant/economics streamer famous for understanding NPC markets | player–NPC trade mediation and commercial policy. |
| **HearthDad** | Kenji Sato, Japan | Human | settlement-roleplay organiser whose guild schedules looked comically over-serious pre-Lock | municipal administration and civilian settlement organisation. |
| **Inevitable** | Marcus Greene, United States | Golem | high-concept “become Thanos” relic-roleplayer; obsessed with assembling absurd magical artefacts | relic seeker whose joke premise becomes increasingly less funny once artefacts have real power. |
| **QuietThunder** | Nandi Mbeki, South Africa | Lupiveni | ranger/hunter, anti-poaching content creator | wildlife management, tracking and ranger organisation. |

## 38.9 Dragonblooded cohort
Only **two** random launch-era players are known to possess the ultra-rare Dragonblooded trait. It does not guarantee that a true dragon will bond with either of them.

### LedgerSaint
- Real name: **Oliver Fenwick**
- Origin: Manchester, England
- Species: Human
- Age at Lock-In: 32
- Earth background: accountant / financial analyst
- Playstyle: trade, logistics, municipal finance, deliberately unglamorous optimisation
- Reputation: niche economics celebrity; repeatedly mocked because his rare mysterious trait appeared on an accountant instead of a raid champion
- Post-Lock niche: fiscal/logistical organiser whose Dragonblooded status makes him politically sensitive far beyond his own ambitions.

### StarlingAsh
- Real name: **Trần Ngọc Lan**
- Origin: Ho Chi Minh City, Vietnam
- Species: Elf
- Age at Lock-In: 23
- Earth background: aerospace-engineering student
- Playstyle: exploration, climbing, flight theory, ruins, dangerous traversal
- Reputation: adventurous creator with a much larger audience than LedgerSaint; intense speculation surrounds her unexplained trait
- Post-Lock niche: explorer and aerial-systems specialist whose Dragonblooded status puts her under enormous scrutiny.

Governments, guilds and scholars know the two traits are exceptional but do **not** initially know which, if any, of the twelve true dragons could ever accept either player.

## 38.10 Roleplay preparation and the post-Lock reversal
Roleplay guilds can be unexpectedly resilient because many spent Year One rehearsing command systems, agriculture, logistics, law, heraldry, councils, military drills and civic ritual for fun. Post-Lock, some discover they accidentally trained for governance.

Likewise, famous noncombat specialists can become more valuable than many celebrity fighters. Food, medicine, engineering, logistics, mapping, administration and agriculture are civilisation-scale needs.

Criminal guilds face the opposite shock: pre-Lock scams, assassination contracts and griefing were bounded by game rules and reversible death. After Lock-In, some dissolve or reform immediately; others become genuine organised crime.

---


# 39. Phase III.H — Player Culture

Player culture is not one culture. With billions of registered accounts and an early post-Lock baseline of roughly 1.65 billion trapped people, Aetheria develops a vast, internally contradictory human diaspora culture with its own slang, memes, fashions, celebrities, ethical disputes, sports, music, roleplay traditions, relationships, religious experiments and post-Lock taboos.

## 39.1 Pre-Lock internet ecosystem
Before Lock-In, Aetheria supports an enormous surrounding online culture: forums, wikis, video platforms, livestreams, build guides, guild sites, social communities, fan art, fanfiction, datamining, drama channels, challenge runs, speedrunning, lore research and creator communities.

Fame is fragmented. A player can be enormous within raiding, crafting, lore, PvP, farming, roleplay, fashion, trading or exploration circles while remaining obscure elsewhere. Only a very small number become globally recognised.

## 39.2 Player naming, local names and epithets
Players have globally unique account/character names, but NPCs interpret those names through local languages and naming conventions.

Examples:
- **IronBishop** may become **Ferris Bishop**, **Ethan Bishop**, a literal local translation, or another culturally plausible derivative depending on region.
- A username already resembling a plausible proper name, such as **Aragorn**, **TonyStark** or **TywinLannister**, may be spoken essentially unchanged by Aetherians.
- Numeric or duplicate-like names create local solutions. If NPCs know both `TywinLannister` and `TywinLannister1`, they may distinguish them through emergent epithets such as **Tywin the Clever**, **Tywin the Foolish**, **Tywin of Westmere**, **the First Tywin** or **the Other Tywin**.

A single player can therefore accumulate an Earth name, username, one or more Aetherian local names, titles and regional epithets. The epithet often reflects how the local population actually experienced that person.

## 39.3 Linguistic exchange
Player jargon naturally enters Aetherian speech over time. Terms such as **world boss**, **aggro**, **tank**, **healer**, **pull**, **wipe**, **main**, **guild**, **quest** and similar MMO vocabulary may become understood by NPCs after prolonged exposure, though often with shifted meanings.

The reverse also occurs. Players borrow Aetherian legal, magical, religious, social and species-specific words when Earth-language equivalents are inadequate.

Pre-Lock automatic Common localisation creates unusually multilingual guilds and friendships. An English-speaking and Japanese-speaking player can both use Common and understand one another seamlessly through background translation. This helps make major guilds much less ethnically/nationally segregated than many Earth online communities.

## 39.4 The NPC taboo
Pre-Lock, players normally call Aetherians **NPCs** because they believe that is what they are.

After Lock-In, the term gradually becomes socially controversial and eventually taboo in many communities. Common alternatives include:
- Aetherian,
- local,
- native,
- citizen,
- or the person's actual nationality/species/community identity.

Some die-hard gamers continue using **NPC**, sometimes out of habit and sometimes deliberately. Aetherians who learn what the term originally meant may regard it as dismissive or dehumanising, although context and individual reaction vary.

The ethical split begins even before Lock-In: some players treat NPCs as simulated people deserving respect, while others exploit, torment or manipulate them as software. Post-Lock, the latter behaviour acquires a much darker reputation.

## 39.5 Roleplay becomes reality-adjacent
Roleplay ranges from casual tavern acting to year-long immersive personas as knights, monks, nobles, merchants, mercenaries, priests and local citizens.

Some NPCs never fully realise a dedicated roleplayer is pretending. Conversely, some roleplayers accumulate genuine:
- debts,
- friendships,
- offices,
- marriages,
- feuds,
- oaths,
- land rights,
- and legal obligations.

After Lock-In, their fictional identity may already have become socially real.

## 39.6 Romance, dating and marriage
Aetheria has extensive pre-Lock dating culture.

Player-player couples often care about Earth distance and language because they hope to meet physically outside the game; many filter romantic partners by real-world region despite Common eliminating the in-game language barrier.

Player-NPC romance also occurs and generates major pre-Lock arguments about whether such relationships are 'real'. After Lock-In, that debate becomes rapidly obsolete.

Marriage is performed through actual Aetherian legal/religious institutions where permitted, not through a universal game marriage button. Player-player, player-Aetherian and cross-species marriage depend on local law and custom.

Biological compatibility is not guaranteed by marriage. In particular, Human–Golem pairings and most Human–Beastfolk pairings cannot produce biological children; **Satyrs are an established exception among Beastfolk**. Other cross-species reproductive rules remain a separate biological canon question unless already specified elsewhere.

## 39.7 Religion and imported Earth belief
Player religious responses span:
- sincere conversion to Threefold traditions,
- atheism/agnosticism,
- comparative theology,
- treating the gods as lore,
- aggressive debate with clergy,
- meme religions,
- attempts to introduce Earth religions.

Some sincerely religious players attempt missionary work among Aetherians. Most Aetherians are sceptical when imported faiths cannot demonstrate the kinds of publicly observable miracles, soul phenomena or divine traditions they already know, but small communities and syncretic cults can still form.

The observable existence of souls and genuine miraculous phenomena does not automatically settle broader theological questions.

## 39.8 Cultural leakage from Earth media
Players constantly reference Earth fiction, history and memes that Aetherians do not understand. Many try to explain them.

Over time this creates **cultural contamination** in the benign anthropological sense: local storytellers can absorb Earth tales, misunderstand them, retell them, merge them with local history and eventually produce traditions that look bizarrely authentic.

A future chronicle might therefore contain a localised legend about **Gandalf the Grey** as though he were an ancient wandering wizard of some remote kingdom.

Travelling bards can likewise learn Earth songs from players and spread them beyond the player's control. Players generally attempt to respect Earth copyright rules pre-Lock, but once an NPC hears, memorises and reteaches a song, legal control becomes functionally impossible inside the simulation.

Known cultural-leak examples/flags include:
- **The Rains of Castamere** becoming known among some bards through player performance.
- **Long Night Ahead** by Colm R. McGuinness becoming especially popular in Aldemerian tavern culture.
- traditional Aetherian songs such as **The Snows of Kryfehold** and **Lament for Heward** becoming major favourites among players and Earth fandom in return.

No copyrighted lyrics are canonically reproduced in this backup; only the existence and cultural spread of the songs is established.

## 39.9 Fashion and cosplay
Player fashion develops independently of combat optimisation. Trends include regional NPC dress, armour silhouettes, jewellery, cloaks, dyes, species-specific accessories, deliberately impractical formal wear and streamer-driven crazes.

Players also cosplay Earth fictional archetypes inside Aetheria. Legally-distinct approximations of space marines, witchers, demon hunters, masked sci-fi warriors, superheroes and other pop-culture figures become common enough that some NPCs assume these represent genuine Earth military/religious orders.

The running community joke **'IS THAT ACTUALLY HENRY CAVILL PLAYING THE WITCHER?'** survives as the optional **Cavill Watch** Easter egg. Henry Cavill's actual participation remains deliberately provisional rather than awaiting another Phase-V decision; do not make the cameo mandatory unless explicitly locked later.

## 39.10 Sports and competitions
Players create and import a wide range of competitive activities:
- horse racing,
- wyvern racing where Wyvernblooded participation makes it possible,
- sailing,
- archery,
- jousting,
- magical duelling,
- parkour,
- cooking contests,
- crafting competitions,
- obstacle courses,
- endurance races,
- and player-created absurd sports.

Modern Earth sports such as association football/soccer, rugby, basketball, cricket, baseball and others are introduced to NPC communities. Adoption varies by region; rules are often locally modified to suit Aetherian physiology, magic, terrain and cultural tastes.

## 39.11 Griefing, trolling and behaviour shift
Pre-Lock culture includes ordinary online-game nonsense:
- blocking doors,
- luring creatures,
- fake treasure maps,
- intentionally awful bard performances,
- streamer baiting,
- pointless guild feuds,
- bait-and-switch pranks,
- PvP trolling.

Developers police severe harassment and exploit abuse, but ordinary mischief is common.

Post-Lock, norms shift rapidly once injuries, property loss, fear and death are real. Many formerly harmless jokes become assault, fraud, endangerment or murder.

A recurring generational joke is the **Grandpa Rage-Quit Incident** archetype: an elderly player trying to relax while an eighteen-year-old on another continent relentlessly trolls them until they log off—funny pre-Lock, impossible in the same way afterward.

## 39.12 Player cultural stereotypes
These are **stereotypes, not objective biological truths**.

Common jokes include:
- Humans = 'default settings'.
- Dark Elves = edgy players.
- Elves = beautiful min-maxers or lore snobs depending on who is speaking.
- Gatans/Lupiveni = 'definitely not furries' said by people who are absolutely going to keep arguing about it.
- Satyrs = party players.
- Golems = tanks, builders and people who enjoy being impossible to move.
- Automatons = spreadsheet addicts and engineers.
- Nymphs = people who refuse to leave the coastline.
- Averi = movement/flight nerds.

Creative Assembly crossover culture also produces recurring **Zerkovich-style 'stinky elves' jokes**, with Dark Elves becoming the nearest available bonking target when actual Total War-style elves are not around.

Real-world nationality jokes also persist, but Common's language bridging makes national segregation relatively weak. Australians become particularly associated with treating Zakhar's venomous ecology with suspicious familiarity.

## 39.13 The canonical culture package — 40 recurring phenomena

### Names, language and etiquette
1. **Name Drift** — NPCs regionalise usernames into culturally plausible personal names.
2. **The Tywin Problem** — duplicate/pop-culture usernames are distinguished through emergent epithets rather than database suffixes.
3. **Epithet Yelp** — the joke that if NPCs give you a title, it is effectively the world's most permanent public review of your behaviour.
4. **Common Brain** — player slang for forgetting that the engine is translating Common between Earth languages in the background.
5. **First-Speech Flexing** — players showing off genuine learned Aetherian languages after relying on translation for months.
6. **The NPC Question** — the long-running ethics dispute over whether 'NPC' is acceptable; post-Lock it evolves toward a widespread taboo.
7. **No Quest Marker** — phrase used when a player realises they must actually ask people, investigate or read documents rather than follow UI guidance.

### Memes and forum folklore
8. **Silverbelly** — shorthand for compulsively checking bizarre possibilities because one improbable discovery happened once; originates with the wolf containing swallowed coins.
9. **The Elf-Mommy Expedition** — pre-Lock meme format about groups of comically overconfident players travelling to Eshv'alara to court impossibly glamorous Elven women, usually paired with ridiculous 'mogging' imagery. Mostly dies out after Lock-In as relationships and consequences become real.
10. **Pet It?** — recurring meme attached to obviously dangerous fauna; spawned by repeated early incidents of players approaching creatures because they looked cute.
11. **Stinky Elves** — Creative Assembly/Zerkovich-adjacent crossover meme aimed particularly at Dark Elves.
12. **Inevitable Posting** — whenever player Inevitable acquires another absurd magical relic, communities post increasingly worried mock 'Infinity Stone' comparisons.
13. **Boss Music Syndrome** — players joking that an encounter is doomed when dramatic music starts. Vorthraktalyrionth's potential awareness/annoyance at system-level music remains an **intentional unresolved hidden-lore possibility**, not a generally established true-dragon ability.
14. **The Forum Expert** — mocking term for players who speak with absolute certainty about undocumented mechanics and are later proven hilariously wrong.
15. **World-Boss Tourism** — players travelling enormous distances simply to see famous creatures from a theoretically safe vantage point.

### Earth culture leaking into Aetheria
16. **The Gandalf Effect** — Earth fictional characters slowly becoming misremembered as historical or mythic figures in local Aetherian storytelling.
17. **Bard Leakage** — songs learned from players spreading uncontrollably through NPC oral culture.
18. **Aldemerian Long Night** — tavern popularity of *Long Night Ahead* becomes a recognisable player/Aldemerian crossover tradition.
19. **Castamere Contamination** — bardic circulation of *The Rains of Castamere* creates generations of Aetherians with no idea what a 'Castamere' originally was.
20. **Earthsong Nights** — taverns hosting evenings where players teach Earth folk songs and NPCs teach Aetherian ones.

### Fashion, celebrity and creator culture
21. **Wisteria-core** — player fashion inspired by Vaelorian university aesthetics, layered robes, academic insignia and literal wisteria motifs.
22. **Knightcore** — Aldemerian/Fahtari martial fashion worn by people with absolutely no intention of fighting.
23. **Cosplay Orders** — NPC nickname for clusters of players dressed as recognisable Earth fictional archetypes; NPCs sometimes believe they are actual Earth orders.
24. **The Famous Hat Problem** — streamer wears an obscure NPC-made hat; demand explodes; local tailor accidentally becomes wealthy.
25. **Chat Did This** — generic phrase for livestream disasters caused by audience voting. DougDoug's canonical Year-One streams are the flagship example, including chat-driven absurdity that later feeds into the Chat Charter and the infamous fake-chat deception during Lock-In.
26. **Cavill Watch** — running joke whenever an unusually convincing Witcher-like cosplayer appears; may become a genuine celebrity cameo only if Phase V explicitly canonises it.

### Relationships and social life
27. **Region Check** — pre-Lock dating etiquette where players disclose broad Earth region before getting serious because they may want an offline relationship too.
28. **The NPC Romance War** — enormous pre-Lock forum argument over whether romance with an Aetherian 'counts'; becomes painfully obsolete post-Lock.
29. **Roleplay Marriage Surprise** — people who married through actual local law 'for the bit' discover that the magistrate, family and community regard the marriage as entirely real.
30. **The Biology Lecture** — recurring post-Lock joke after cross-species couples discover that romance and reproductive compatibility are separate questions.

### Sport and competition
31. **Common Football** — association football becomes one of the easiest Earth sports to export because the basic concept requires minimal technology, though local variants multiply rapidly.
32. **Aerial Circuit** — Wyvernblooded racing tradition combining speed, navigation and animal partnership; heavily regulated because crashes are not funny after Lock-In.
33. **Forge Trials** — spectator crafting competitions in which smiths/engineers produce items under constraints and are judged by actual professionals.
34. **Tavern Games Renaissance** — Earth card, dice, pub and trivia traditions merge with Aetherian games and become ubiquitous social entertainment.

### Religion, trolling and social norms
35. **Missionary Threads** — endless pre-Lock debates where Earth religious players discuss whether and how to proselytise to Aetherians; most attempts remain small because Aetherians already live in a world with observable soul/miracle phenomena.
36. **Meme Cults** — joke religions occasionally acquire sincere adherents, much to everybody's discomfort.
37. **Doorway Criminalisation** — blocking an NPC doorway goes from classic griefing to behaviour that can get somebody physically dragged away by guards after Lock-In.
38. **The Respawn Reflex** — early post-Lock habit of players using pre-Lock risk assumptions and then abruptly remembering there is no respawn.

### Commemoration
39. **Lock-In Day** — neutral/common umbrella term for the anniversary event; commemorations vary from mourning to survival celebration.
40. **Second Birth / Empty Chair / The Crossing** — competing post-Lock traditions: some communities celebrate the day as a second life, some place empty chairs for Earth/lost bodies and dead players, and some later illegal entrants treat their chosen entry date as a personal migration anniversary called a Crossing.

## 39.14 Culture is dynamic, not scripted
These phenomena are seeds, not static commandments. Memes can die, mutate or become incomprehensible. Player slang can enter NPC languages. Aetherian songs can become Earth fandom staples. Earth stories can be localised beyond recognition. Guild jokes can become political slogans. Once Lock-In removes the external internet and makes consequences real, cultural selection accelerates: practices useful for survival persist, while many purely pre-Lock memes disappear within years.

The setting should preserve the difference between:
- **pre-Lock internet culture**,
- **early trapped-player culture**,
- **mature post-Lock Aetherian-human culture**.

---

# 40. Phase III.I — The First Year

## 40.1 Launch scale and staggered opening
Aetheria Online launches first in the United States, Canada, Australia, United Kingdom, New Zealand, Ireland, Japan, South Korea, China, Thailand, and Vietnam. Approximately **100 million accounts** are created in the initial launch period. Entry is staggered by authentication/load-management rather than all players appearing at one instant.

The four principal starter centres are:
- **Westmere** in Eldrath — classic approachable fantasy/frontier experience;
- **Stoneskip** in Vaeloria — cosmopolitan magical/coastal start;
- **Estopolis** in Estermorion — the highest-capacity metropolitan starter hub and a major pressure valve for launch population;
- **Phoralier** in the Azure Archipelago — maritime/Nymphic-focused start.

Starts are recommended by desired experience/species suitability but are not rigidly race-locked.

### The Quarter-Million Mis-Spawn
A launch routing bug affects roughly **250,000 players** (~0.25% of the initial 100 million accounts). They appear outside intended starter areas: advanced cities, politically hostile jurisdictions, wilderness, monster territories, restricted buildings, or other absurd locations. Many immediately die and respawn under the pre-Lock system; others are arrested, rescued, or accidentally become early explorers.

The most famous single incident is the **Royal Bedroom Spawn**, in which one unfortunate new player appears inside **Queen Sael'ira Balv'alim's private bedroom** in Eshv'alara. Sael'ira rapidly concludes that the terrified, minimally equipped foreigner is more likely a system-displaced idiot than an assassin. The incident becomes one of launch week's defining memes and one of the earliest examples of an NPC responding intelligently to an impossible player event.

## 40.2 Diegetic tutorial system
Tutorials are primarily delivered through actual Aetherian people and institutions, supported by pre-Lock UI prompts. Dedicated tutorial NPCs exist, but **do not know they are tutorial NPCs**. They understand themselves as militia instructors, civic guides, healers, tradespeople, navigators, clerks, priests, or patient local officials assigned to teach bewildered foreigners.

Core tutorials cover movement, combat, injury/death/respawn, money, local law, languages/translation, physical carrying/equipment management, professions, travel, NPC interaction, and later guild formation. The system deliberately encourages players to engage with society rather than complete an abstract floating-menu obstacle course.

## 40.3 Guild race
No player begins in a guild. Guild registration unlocks after the core tutorial and requires:
- several founding members (normally at least five);
- a unique registered name;
- a governance/charter declaration;
- a recognised administrative contact point;
- **substantial capital/registration cost in locally recognised currency or Argent-equivalent**;
- sufficient local/system prestige to demonstrate that the founders are more than a brand-new party.

This causes a frantic early race to accumulate money, reputation, and legal standing. Many players attempt names such as “FirstGuild” only to discover that someone else registered them earlier. Guilds therefore emerge socially and economically rather than at character creation.

## 40.4 Early player behaviour and the Harthwick scandal
The first month is the worst period for treating Aetherians as disposable software. Players test pathfinding, steal objects, repeat dialogue, block doors, provoke guards, and attack people simply to determine system responses.

The first major ethics scandal becomes known as the **Harthwick Loop**. A player group discovers that they can repeatedly provoke a crisis around a small Aldemerian settlement, exploit the resulting reward/economic conditions, die/respawn when resistance escalates, and return. The crucial discovery is that the inhabitants **remember**. They become frightened, angry, economically damaged, and eventually alter behaviour to resist/avoid the players. Developers patch the exploitable loop and sanction involved accounts, but do not simply erase the social consequences.

The event creates the first enormous public argument over whether cruelty toward highly persistent simulated people should be treated as “just gameplay.” It materially accelerates the emergence of pro-Aetherian etiquette before Lock-In.

## 40.5 Pain, death and the Respawn Era
Pre-Lock full-dive VR simulates physical sensation and pain strongly enough that injury is unpleasant and often frightening even though death is reversible. Players therefore receive genuine negative feedback from reckless behaviour, but respawn still produces a culture of extraordinary stupidity.

Common Year-One practices include:
- suicidal scouting to learn boss behaviour;
- deliberate fatal falls to test traversal;
- respawn shortcuts;
- reckless poison/fire testing;
- impossible boss attempts for content;
- trying to determine whether water behaves like Minecraft water.

It does not.

The resulting injuries hurt. This discourages some players but by no means all of them. The behaviour later explains the dangerous early post-Lock **Respawn Reflex**.

## 40.6 The Iron Squeeze and Ferrum's player economy
Launch demand shocks the simulated economy. Tens of millions of players simultaneously want iron, leather, beginner weapons, healing supplies, horses, rooms, food, land, and crafting materials. Prices surge in several starter regions; local NPC consumers can temporarily be priced out of ordinary goods.

Ferrum Argentarium initially regards players as extremely strange foreigners with immense aggregate purchasing power, poor budgeting, inconsistent risk perception, and a talent for creating novel forms of fraud. During Year One Ferrum develops dedicated player-facing exchange/account services, fraud models, debt recovery procedures, and cross-border credit products.

Players who borrow irresponsibly learn that Ferrum treats debt as an actual obligation. Respawning does not void a loan. Changing town does not void a loan. Crossing a border does not necessarily void a loan. Ferrum possesses institutional patience measured in years.

## 40.7 Age of Discovery
Months 1–6 are dominated by an enormous discovery race. Players map roads, ruins, languages, species, politics, economies, hidden mechanics, magical principles, monster ecology, and history. This period produces or rapidly expands organisations such as **Atlas Union**, **Living Archive Project**, lore communities, mapping cooperatives, and player academic circles.

The public wiki becomes enormous but never omniscient. Rumour, bad translation, staged content, local folklore, deliberate misinformation, mistaken boss identities, and undiscovered regional variation produce constant corrections and forum wars.

## 40.8 Famous Year-One boss events
True world bosses remain overwhelmingly alive. Minor and major bosses, however, produce several famous first kills.

1. **Gravelmaw of Westmere** — oversized Greyhorn Boar; killed on Day 9 by an improvised 23-player hunt. Usually remembered as the first widely broadcast named-boss kill.
2. **The Shardjaw of Stoneskip** — exceptionally large Glassjaw monster; killed in Month 2 after repeated failed attempts teach players that anatomical damage matters more than conventional DPS assumptions.
3. **Redwater Crown** — enormous Redwater Maw in Zakhar; killed in Month 5 by a mixed hunter/medical/logistics expedition rather than a conventional raid.
4. **The Seven-Limbed Butcher** — aberrant magical predator in Vaeloria; killed in Month 6 after players identify that one apparently redundant limb contains an essential magical organ.
5. **Cradle-Eater** — unusually old Gorebrooder in Choreleth; killed in Month 8 by a coalition considered under-equipped for the attempt. Its carcass generates one of the first major disputes over monster-part ownership and preservation.
6. **Ash-King Rhor** — enormous Smoke Hound pack leader in Drakoria; killed in Month 10 after repeated expeditions learn to exploit its dependence on volcanic-gas terrain.

The most famous non-kill is **Black Ruin Weekend**: a huge Month-7 player coalition attempts to overwhelm **Porferion** through numbers. Porferion survives. Thousands of players are killed/respawned, formations collapse under concussive breath, and the footage becomes an enduring lesson that a world boss is not obliged to be fair merely because many players turned up.

Developers deliberately refuse to publish guaranteed loot tables for unique legendary creatures. When players ask what Porferion drops, the functional answer is: **nobody knows; Porferion is still alive**.

## 40.9 Quarterly crossover cycle
Aetheria establishes a major licensed crossover every roughly three months during Year One. These are developer-authored events/cosmetics/challenges inside Aetheria and are **not evidence that the external fictional settings literally exist cosmologically**.

- **Month 3 — Creative Assembly:** first major corporate crossover. Mass-battle events, strategy competitions, heraldic/cosmetic content, and the culture that produces the **Grand Warhost**. Creative Assembly becomes remembered as the company that proved Aetheria crossovers could be enormous social events.
- **Month 6 — Bungie:** science-fantasy themed event content, cooperative challenges, rare technology-styled cosmetics/props, and intense lore speculation around how much of the event is “real” to Aetherians.
- **Month 9 — Gaijin:** military-engineering/vehicle-history themed competitions, reconstruction challenges, ballistic experimentation and player engineering culture.
- **Month 12 — Blizzard:** anniversary headline crossover and final major partnership of Year One, overlapping the enormous first-anniversary festivities.

## 40.10 Creator/streamer era
Months 2–10 are Aetheria's first creator golden age. In the fictional 2057 timeline, the following creator appearances are established canonical Year-One viral moments:

- **DougDoug** lets Twitch chat determine major choices, travels to Eshv'alara, and—after a poll—asks **Queen Sael'ira** to marry him. She declines with amused political composure. The clip becomes one of the most-viewed Aetheria streams of Year One.
- **IShowSpeed** becomes increasingly exasperated by players deliberately trolling, baiting and griefing his streams, producing a long-running sequence of viral rage clips.
- **Jynxzi** encounters a true dragon for the first time on-stream and completely loses composure; the reaction becomes the default meme clip for “player sees something above their level.”
- **CaseOh** discovers that his character is **Wyvernblooded**. His existing audience's running jokes follow him into Aetheria, and even sufficiently socially bold NPCs occasionally join the teasing. More importantly, he later becomes the **first publicly verified player to form a genuine soul-bond with an Enclave wyvern**, turning what began as a comedy-stream reveal into major game history.

CaseOh's first bonded wyvern is provisionally **Vhalemyr**, a young Enclave-bred fire-line wyvern whose bond is accepted by Enclave handlers as genuine. This does not make CaseOh superhuman or automatically an elite rider; it makes him the first player who has to learn the profession for real.

## 40.11 Body modification discoveries
Because character creation is biometrically constrained, Year One players progressively discover that Aetherian medicine, alchemy, technology and magic permit far greater later modification.

The discovery curve runs from cosmetics, scars, hair/skin/voice alteration and minor anatomical changes into advanced procedures capable of changing sex or even species. These are **real embodied procedures**, not menu toggles. Advanced alteration can be painful, medically dangerous, expensive, and dependent on anaesthesia, healing magic and competent practitioners. Pre-Lock respawn reduces some permanent risk but does not make the procedures painless.

The first well-documented advanced transformations become major medical and cultural events, generating enormous demand, ethics debates, misinformation and amateur attempts that competent healers strongly discourage.

## 40.12 Wyvernblooded revelation
At launch most players interpret **Wyvernblooded** as an obscure rare trait affecting resistance, balance, reflexes or draconic flavour. Through Year One, expeditions to Drakoria and contact with the Enclave reveal that it is actually the necessary soul/physiological basis for genuine wyvern bonding.

CaseOh and Vhalemyr provide the first highly public proof. Thereafter the roughly 1% of players with the trait face intense interest, recruitment, speculation and scams promising “guaranteed compatible eggs.” The Enclave repeatedly explains that Wyvernblooded status does **not** guarantee soul compatibility with any given wyvern.

## 40.13 Dragonblooded mystery
**LedgerSaint** and **StarlingAsh** possess the vastly rarer **Dragonblooded** trait. Developers refuse to explain it publicly. Dataminers find almost no reliable documentation. Community theories range from hidden prestige class to dragon transformation to developer joke.

Neither forms a true-dragon bond during Year One. The relationship between Dragonblooded players and the twelve true dragons remains unresolved when Lock-In occurs.

## 40.14 The Chat Charter — first major player political recognition
DougDoug accidentally becomes associated with another major first. After the failed Sael'ira proposal, later streams repeatedly return to Eshv'alara. Twitch chat pushes him toward progressively more absurd civic/political choices rather than combat objectives.

A long chain of road-restoration, trade, dispute-resolution and local-service actions eventually produces what players nickname the **Chat Charter**: Sael'ira's government grants DougDoug's organised player community formal rights/responsibilities over a small player-administered waystation/quarter under Eshv'alara law.

This is not sovereignty, but it is widely recognised as the first highly public case of a player organisation receiving **serious governmental legal recognition, land-use authority and civic obligations** rather than merely owning a house or taking a mercenary contract. Political guilds immediately realise the precedent matters.

## 40.15 Final quarter and anniversary build-up
By Months 9–12 Aetheria is no longer culturally new. It has:
- megaguilds;
- established player businesses;
- player–NPC marriages;
- university students researching game phenomena;
- mature economic relationships with Ferrum and local states;
- famous streamers and celebrities;
- crossovers and competitive circuits;
- thousands of persistent settlements/organisations;
- enormous wikis, forums and creator archives;
- increasingly normal Aetherian adoption of player slang and culture.

The first anniversary is marketed as Aetheria's largest event yet. Returning-player incentives, the Blizzard crossover, concerts, tournaments, guild events, discovery celebrations, streamer broadcasts and anniversary bonuses pull enormous numbers of inactive/occasional users back online.

Normal monthly active population immediately beforehand is ~750 million, but anniversary concurrency reaches approximately **875 million**.

## 40.16 The final evening
The immediate atmosphere before Lock-In is deliberately, cruelly normal.

Players are:
- meeting friends and partners;
- preparing raids;
- attending concerts;
- running anniversary races;
- showing returning friends what changed;
- travelling to favourite taverns;
- streaming;
- negotiating contracts;
- doing ordinary profession work;
- celebrating one year of a game many now consider a second home.

Aetherian taverns and public squares are crowded with foreigners celebrating an anniversary many locals only partly understand. There is **no reliable apocalyptic warning**, blood moon, prophetic global event or obvious doomsday countdown.

The anniversary message is essentially:

> **Happy First Anniversary, Aetheria Online.**

Then the Lock-In occurs.

The reason its exact timing is globally inconvenient and apparently somewhat random belongs to later hidden-lore work.

---


# 41. III.J — Post-Lock-In Population & Society

## 41.1 The Lock-In is discovered, not announced
Lock-In does **not** begin with a system proclamation explaining the truth. The anniversary simply stops behaving like a game.

Players notice anomalies in different orders:
- **Log Out** is gone;
- respawn controls vanish;
- fast travel ceases to function;
- pain and physical sensation become much more consequential;
- hunger, thirst, fatigue and other bodily needs stop being background abstractions;
- the automatic language/localisation layer disappears;
- many HUD conveniences vanish or shrink;
- deaths stop resolving through the familiar respawn cycle.

The first theories are mundane by Aetheria standards: anniversary event, catastrophic patch, experimental immersion mode, mass server fault, malicious hack, or developer stunt. There is no trustworthy global narrator telling everyone what has happened.

The phrase **“I don't think this is a patch”** becomes one of the characteristic early-Lock sayings.

## 41.2 The residual interface — restored prior canon
Lock-In does **not** erase every trace of the game interface. The system retains the bare minimum necessary for players to recognise that they still inhabit Aetheria's underlying game/simulation framework.

Removed or heavily reduced:
- conventional health-bar convenience;
- soul-strain/stamina HUD convenience where it acted as omniscient instrumentation;
- floating names/nameplates;
- quest arrows and objective markers;
- omniscient minimap information;
- damage numbers;
- enemy level/difficulty labels;
- conventional chat/subtitles/auto-translation;
- Fast Travel;
- Respawn;
- Log Out;
- Delete Character.

Retained in **minimal** form:
- current species and broad bodily condition;
- known skills and traits;
- achievements/history records;
- map interfaces, limited to legitimately known/mapped information rather than omniscient revelation;
- guild/organisation interfaces where those functions still correspond to real persistent organisations;
- essential system notifications/messages.

The Ghost HUD does **not** automatically display the user's own name, age, titles, affiliations or other social identity data. Those facts must be remembered, inferred, documented or learned in-world like any other social information.

There was **never** an abstract inventory grid to remove. Aetheria has always treated possessions physically: weapons are worn or carried, food occupies bags or containers, armour has weight and bulk, and cargo needs storage and transport. Lock-In therefore intensifies the consequences of physical possession but does not abolish a dimensionless inventory because none existed.

Players often call this surviving layer the **residual interface** or, jokingly, the **ghost HUD**. It is enough to remind them that reality still has a system beneath it, but nowhere near enough to make survival feel like playing a normal MMO.

## 41.3 The first permanent deaths
The first confirmed permanent player deaths occur within minutes to hours, because some players instinctively repeat actions that were merely inconvenient before Lock-In: fatal shortcuts, stunt jumps, reckless monster pulls, suicide scouting, or dangerous experimentation.

At first bystanders often wait for the dead player to respawn.

Nothing happens.

The body remains.

Because communication has fractured, this revelation spreads unevenly. One city can know death is permanent while players a few hundred kilometres away are still performing pre-Lock stunts. The old **Respawn Reflex** becomes one of the most dangerous pieces of learned behaviour in the early crisis.

## 41.4 Player corpses and funerals
Post-Lock dead players leave **physical corpses**. Their possessions remain with them unless removed, and the body undergoes the same anatomical, magical, legal and funerary consequences as any other dead person in Aetheria.

This is psychologically shocking because a pre-Lock player death culminated in a respawn rather than a persistent body.

Within days, player communities begin improvising:
- identification systems;
- morgues;
- burial/cremation arrangements according to local law and personal belief;
- missing-person records;
- inheritance procedures;
- death notices;
- body-recovery teams.

The **Order of the Closed Mouth** becomes particularly important. Its members already possess cultural, religious and practical expertise in treating the dead as people rather than discarded game objects.

## 41.5 Earth-body uncertainty
The original trapped cohort does **not** immediately know that their minds have been uploaded and their biological Earth bodies are wasting away and dying.

Early theories include:
- coma or paralysis;
- neural lock;
- server imprisonment;
- consciousness copying rather than transfer;
- mass hallucination;
- a sophisticated hostage situation;
- divine intervention;
- alien or unknown technology;
- the possibility that they remain physically connected to Earth and can eventually wake.

This uncertainty preserves hope of returning home for a significant early period. Definitive outside information arrives later through surviving communication channels and, especially, people who enter after the consequences have become known on Earth.

The distinction between **“we might be able to go home”** and **“there is no living body to return to”** becomes one of the most devastating psychological transitions in post-Lock history.

## 41.6 The language collapse
Pre-Lock auto-localisation made Common effectively transparent. An English-language player and Japanese-language player could each hear Common in their own Earth language while still conversing normally.

At Lock-In this convenience disappears.

Players hear **actual Aetherian languages**.

Therefore an English-speaking player and Japanese-speaking player who previously spoke effortlessly can suddenly fail to understand one another unless:
- both genuinely know Common;
- one knows the other's Earth language through some other means;
- they use a translator/interpreter;
- they rely temporarily on gesture, writing, or limited magical assistance.

Actual language knowledge survives. Players who invested in Common or other Aetherian languages become disproportionately valuable overnight. NPC interpreters, language teachers, dictionaries, notebooks, multilingual guild members and the **Common Tongue Society** become critical infrastructure.

The crisis also has a long-term consequence: hundreds of millions of Earth humans are forced to learn **Common as an actual second language**, making it the dominant bridge language of post-Lock player civilisation.

## 41.7 Bodies become fully lived-in
Post-Lock player bodies require genuine bodily maintenance.

The following become real rather than lightly abstracted background simulation:
- hunger;
- thirst;
- sleep;
- fatigue;
- temperature exposure;
- illness and infection;
- hygiene;
- excretion;
- menstruation where physiologically applicable;
- wound care;
- nutrition;
- medication or in-world medical equivalents where needed;
- pregnancy and childbirth where biologically possible.

Players already experienced pain and sensation pre-Lock, so none of this is entirely alien, but the consequences are no longer reset by respawn or safely bounded by a game session.

## 41.8 Ageing and lifespan — restored prior canon
A trapped player's avatar becomes their **actual Aetherian body** and ages according to that body's species and physiology.

Age no longer tracks the lost Earth body's biological clock. A player who entered as an elderly-looking Human remains an elderly Aetherian Human unless transformed/healed, but Earth-specific disease and disability do not automatically persist unless they are represented in the Aetherian body.

Species lifespan matters thereafter:
- Human-bodied players age as Humans;
- Elf-bodied players age according to Elven longevity;
- other species follow their established physiology;
- magical, technological, alchemical, undead or other in-world interventions can extend or alter life, but immortality is never a free system privilege.

This means mixed-species couples eventually confront radically different ageing rates and lifespans.

## 41.9 Fertility, pregnancy and the first post-Lock generation — restored prior canon
Players become **biologically fertile according to their current bodies**, where their species/sex/health permit it.

Consequences include:
- player–player pregnancy and children;
- player–Aetherian families;
- mixed cultural households;
- species-specific reproductive compatibility;
- midwifery, obstetrics and childcare becoming strategically important;
- eventual generations who have never seen Earth.

These children are **not new player accounts** and do not appear through character creation. They are naturally born Aetherian people whose parents happen to originate from Earth.

Previously established compatibility remains binding: Human–Golem and most Human–Beastfolk pairings cannot produce biological children, with **Satyrs** an established Beastfolk exception. Other specific interspecies compatibility remains to be formalised rather than assumed.

Within a generation, children begin asking parents and grandparents to describe a world they themselves can never visit.

## 41.10 The first great crisis is logistics
The greatest immediate threat to the Lock-In population is not one monster or war. It is the sudden conversion of hundreds of millions of transient game visitors into **permanent residents with bodies**.

Starter regions face:
- food shortages and distribution failures;
- water/sanitation pressure;
- insufficient housing;
- overcrowding;
- medical overload;
- fires and waste-management problems;
- theft and panic buying;
- transport congestion;
- currency and price shocks;
- pressure on nearby farmland and ecosystems.

**Estopolis** has the best metropolitan capacity to absorb the initial shock and still struggles enormously. Westmere, Stoneskip and Phoralier become major refugee/logistics hubs almost overnight.

Aetherian governments are confronted with a migration/refugee emergency on a scale no local society has ever experienced.

## 41.11 Aetherians discover player mortality
NPC commanders and civilians do not instantly understand that the old respawn cycle has ended.

Over roughly the first **one to two weeks**, accumulating evidence becomes impossible to dismiss:
- bodies remain dead;
- hospitals contain injuries that do not reset;
- grieving guilds bury friends;
- familiar players fail to return;
- repeated battlefield observations confirm mortality.

Aetherian policy changes accordingly.

Militaries stop assuming players are expendable auxiliaries. Murder investigations begin treating player victims as permanently dead people. Rescue priorities change. Prisons and contracts become vastly more meaningful. Some Aetherians are horrified by memories of watching players casually die and return during the previous year.

## 41.12 Players do not automatically conquer Aetheria
Despite enormous numbers and Earth knowledge, players begin the crisis structurally weak.

Advantages:
- population scale;
- enormous diversity of Earth knowledge;
- specialised guilds and professional communities;
- unusual technical and scientific perspectives;
- experience with game systems and some geography;
- many established personal relationships with Aetherians.

Disadvantages:
- most are not trained soldiers;
- the translation layer has collapsed;
- many are concentrated around starter regions;
- they do not own enough farmland or housing;
- their supply chains are shattered;
- most have no recognised sovereignty;
- existing states already possess armies, bureaucracies, roads, ports, granaries, courts and tax systems;
- players still misunderstand enormous amounts of local law, magic, ecology and politics.

The first months are therefore dominated more by **negotiation, dependency and cooperation** than instant player conquest.

## 41.13 Divergent legal treatment
States respond according to their existing institutions and ideology rather than one global solution.

Broad tendencies include:
- **Estermorion** — emergency residency and citizenship pathways, legal aid, public administration and constitutional debate;
- **Aldemere** — protections and settlement charters negotiated separately through the Four Crowns and local authorities;
- **Eshv'alara** — negotiated incorporation through matrilineal/Elven institutions, treaties and local obligations;
- **Feragia** — pragmatic settlement and protection tied strongly to duties, logistics and defence;
- **Pearl Archipelago/Ferrum sphere** — aggressive development of accounts, credit, housing, labour and commercial services for the enormous new permanent market;
- **Epsilaphtocratoria** — ideological registration, surveillance and attempts to control/classify foreign populations;
- **Zhabura** — serious risk of player enslavement or coercive exploitation where vulnerable groups lack protection;
- **Gor'oxuer'funro** — potentially catastrophic treatment including slavery, experimentation and forced undead exploitation;
- **Suralian Isles** — recruitment, opportunism, piracy, settlement bargaining and a very loose concept of immigration paperwork.

Migration between Aetherian jurisdictions rapidly becomes one of the defining political questions of post-Lock society.

## 41.14 Crime is no longer gameplay — restored prior canon
Actions once bounded by game mechanics become ordinary real crimes under local law:
- killing becomes murder or lawful/unlawful homicide depending on circumstance;
- PvP assaults become assault;
- scams can become fraud;
- theft of physically carried/stored possessions remains ordinary theft;
- forced detention becomes kidnapping;
- protection rackets become extortion;
- dungeon looting can become trespass, poaching, grave robbery or unlawful excavation;
- harassment and dangerous griefing can produce actual injury or death.

“It's just PvP” rapidly stops functioning as a meaningful defence.

Many pre-Lock criminal guilds dissolve, reform, or prohibit lethal work. Others become genuine organised-crime networks. NPC criminals, by contrast, already understood permanent violence and sometimes adapt faster than player criminals do.

## 41.15 Guilds become survival infrastructure — restored prior canon
Guild membership rises from approximately half the active population before Lock-In toward a long-term norm around **90%** because guilds become practical mutual-aid institutions.

Large guilds can provide:
- food procurement and rationing;
- shelter;
- medical care;
- translators;
- employment;
- guards and rescue teams;
- childcare;
- burial funds;
- relocation support;
- legal representation;
- credit and equipment sharing;
- education;
- conflict mediation.

This produces a brutal promotion for many guild leaders: raid organisers become administrators responsible for tens or hundreds of thousands of lives.

**Hearthbound** becomes famously well positioned because its supposedly excessive roleplay already included constitutions, agricultural schedules, sanitation, councils, militia structures, taxation and civic planning.

Guild membership is not automatically benevolent. Some organisations become paternalistic, exploitative, coercive, cultic or authoritarian precisely because desperate people depend on them.

## 41.16 Player states emerge slowly
Actual player sovereignty generally takes **months to years**, sometimes longer.

The normal trajectory is:
> guild/community → settlement → recognised charter/municipality → autonomous territory → possible sovereign state.

Existing Aetherian governments usually retain legal authority at first. Recognition can come through purchase, charter, treaty, service, political breakdown, conquest followed by recognition, or other legitimate/illegitimate historical processes.

Some player organisations deliberately pursue democracy. Others become corporate polities, military governments, cooperatives, communes, theocracies or monarchies. Some guild founders discover much too late that “Guildmaster for life” was a funny game rule but an alarming constitutional clause.

A major shortcut exists: **marriage into an Aetherian royal or ruling house**. A politically successful player can gain titles, dynastic claims, influence, land and potentially succession rights far faster than a guild could build independent sovereignty from scratch. This makes royal courtship one of the most consequential—and dangerous—paths to player political power.

## 41.17 Earth identities gradually compete with Aetherian ones
Early generations strongly retain Earth nationality and culture: Australian, Japanese, Nigerian, Brazilian, Canadian, etc.

Over time, other identities become equally or more important:
- Westmerian;
- Eshv'alaran;
- Feragian;
- citizen/member of a player-founded polity;
- Human, Elf, Gatan, Nymph, etc.;
- guild identity;
- local town or regional identity.

“Player” slowly changes from an everyday social category into an **origin/history category**.

Later voluntary entrants are especially important because some arrive already thinking of Aetheria as their chosen homeland rather than an involuntary prison.

## 41.18 Mental health: catastrophe, liberation and everything between
The Lock-In produces no single psychological response.

Common reactions include:
- panic;
- denial;
- acute grief;
- survivor guilt;
- depression;
- anger;
- obsession with returning to Earth;
- religious conversion or crisis;
- identity disturbance;
- reckless risk-taking;
- suicidality;
- euphoria or relief;
- guilt about feeling relieved.

Some disabled or elderly players confront an emotionally complicated reality: they have lost Earth and perhaps family, but their new body may let them walk, run, climb or live without ailments that once limited them.

Some players respond with frankly manic enthusiasm and regard permanent embodiment in Aetheria as an upgrade over ordinary life. Gaming obsessives, fantasy escapists and the proverbial **gacha addict who has somehow won the ultimate isekai pull** become recurring dark-comedy stereotypes, even while the surrounding event remains catastrophic.

Player doctors, psychologists/psychiatrists where available, peer-support communities, NPC healers and Threefold clergy all become crucial. Suicide is no longer followed by respawn.

## 41.19 Later voluntary entrants — the Crossers
The approximately **1.65 billion** trapped after the first two weeks are the early baseline, **not the final all-time player population**.

Once Earth authorities understand that entry is effectively permanent and lethal to the original body, governments attempt to seize, restrict or ban Aetheria hardware and infrastructure. Black markets nevertheless develop.

People continue entering at a much slower rate despite knowing the consequences.

Common motives include:
- reuniting with a spouse, child, parent, sibling or friend;
- choosing permanent migration to Aetheria;
- escaping conditions on Earth;
- religious or ideological conviction;
- fascination with the new world;
- economic/political ambition;
- criminal evasion;
- grief;
- simple obsession.

The most common post-Lock colloquial term becomes **Crossers**, derived from **the Crossing**: the act of knowingly leaving Earth for Aetheria. Formal documents often use terms such as *voluntary entrant* or *post-Lock entrant*.

Crossers occupy a psychologically distinct position because they chose what the original Lock-In population did not.

Typical tensions include:
> “You had a way to stay on Earth and threw it away.”

versus:
> “You were here. I came for you.”

Many Crossers commemorate their individual entry date as **the Crossing**, effectively a personal migration anniversary.

The exact Earth-side black-market mechanisms, laws, company infrastructure and geopolitical response are reserved primarily for Phase V.

## 41.20 The long-term result is migration, not permanent segregation
Player civilisation does **not** remain a sealed foreign layer forever.

Across decades and generations:
- players marry Aetherians;
- naturally born children create mixed families;
- guilds become towns, states, businesses, orders and historical institutions;
- usernames become surnames, noble names, folk heroes or embarrassing ancestral stories;
- Common becomes widespread among Earth-origin communities;
- Earth languages survive through families, schools, archives, religious communities and cultural enclaves;
- Earth sports, stories, technologies, religions and music mutate into Aetherian traditions;
- Aetherian customs, faiths and identities reshape Earth-descended populations;
- “NPC” becomes archaic and often offensive;
- the original Lock-In population becomes a named historical generation rather than a permanent caste.

A child born a century later may describe an ancestor as **“Earthborn”** without thinking of themselves as anything other than Aetherian.

The Lock-In is therefore both a mass catastrophe and the beginning of a **permanent human migration into Aetheria**.

## 41.21 Post-Lock chronology
### Hour 0–6: confusion
- logout and respawn controls disappear;
- translation/localisation fails;
- fast travel ceases;
- scattered permanent deaths occur;
- players initially assume an event or bug;
- residual interface remains just sufficient to show that the underlying system still exists.

### Day 1–3: local realisation
- corpse persistence proves death is different;
- food, water, sleep and sanitation become urgent;
- language barriers fracture many international communities;
- guilds establish emergency shelters and rationing;
- NPC authorities receive contradictory reports;
- rumours about Earth range from hopeful to apocalyptic.

### Day 4–7: emergency organisation
- hospitals, morgues, food depots and interpreter networks expand;
- governments deploy troops/watch forces to manage crowds and crime;
- guild membership surges;
- travel away from overloaded starter centres begins;
- player doctors, logisticians, farmers, engineers and administrators become more valuable than many combat celebrities.

### Week 2: the new reality becomes undeniable
- Aetherian governments broadly accept that players are now mortal;
- roughly **1.65 billion** players have entered by this stage as late anniversary users, worried friends and curious returnees continued logging in before the danger became universally understood;
- legal regimes begin issuing explicit player residency/protection/restriction rules;
- large guilds transition from gaming communities into welfare/governance institutions;
- hope of a quick technical reset begins collapsing.

### Months 1–3: dispersal and adaptation
- mass movement away from overwhelmed starter centres;
- Common-language crash courses become ubiquitous;
- guild economies, hospitals, food systems and neighbourhood administrations solidify;
- criminal networks either professionalise, collapse or become genuinely dangerous;
- marriages, land charters, political patronage and military service become important routes into Aetherian society;
- serious Earth-return and resurrection research accelerates.

### Months 3–12: permanence becomes institutional
- many players function under stable local legal identities;
- player settlements gain municipal charters;
- guild membership trends strongly toward the long-run ~90% affiliation norm;
- Earth professional communities become schools, hospitals, engineering corps, merchant groups and cultural institutions;
- the first Crossers begin to acquire a distinct reputation as knowingly voluntary arrivals;
- some players stop treating “getting home” as their only conceivable future.

### Years 1–5: the first political generation
- a few player settlements develop substantial autonomy;
- royal marriages and court appointments create unusually rapid paths to high political power for individual players;
- early player-governed territories appear, though widespread recognised sovereignty remains uncommon;
- children born after Lock-In become visible symbols that the migration is permanent;
- Earth-national and Aetherian-local identities increasingly overlap rather than exclude one another.

### Decades onward
- player-founded organisations become ordinary historical institutions;
- mixed families and new cultural identities proliferate;
- Earth becomes memory, archive, religion, family story and political symbol rather than lived experience for new generations;
- the distinction between “player society” and “Aetherian society” becomes progressively less useful.

## 41.22 Phase III endpoint
Phase III ends with a population model in which the human arrival does not overwrite Aetheria. Instead, **two immense civilisational populations collide, negotiate, fight, marry, trade, borrow from one another, misunderstand one another and eventually hybridise**.

The initial Lock-In generation remains historically exceptional, but it does not remain socially separate forever.

**Phase III — Population Bible / Players & Community is complete.**

Phase VI formalises the hidden objective reality beneath the public setting: the relation between souls and consciousness, the quantum-metaphysical substrate underlying Aetheria, VRSAGIM's discovery of soul physics, the true continuity-transfer mechanism, the Reynold Buckley precedent, real magic, the planes, the War of the Gods and divine-corpse geography, the Three's inherited portfolios, VRSAGIM's motive for Lock-In, its post-Lock evolution from would-be saviour toward a self-limiting custodian, death/resurrection/return continuity, and the setting's causal rules for discovering hidden truth.

Phase VII formalises how the GPT simulator presents and adjudicates that world. Phase VIII reconciles the accumulated canon and marks genuine unknowns as intentional. **Phase IX is now the only remaining planned design phase: drafting and testing the Final Master Instructions.**


# 45. IV.A — Persistent World State, Time & Causality

## 45.1 Cardinal philosophy: simulation first, narrative second
Aetheria's simulation engine does **not** begin by asking what would make the most dramatic story beat. It begins by asking:

> **Given everything currently true, what is most plausibly happening next?**

Drama should emerge from interacting causes: goals, relationships, resources, geography, institutions, ecology, biology, history, information, chance and player decisions. The simulator may produce cinematic, tragic, absurd, romantic or anticlimactic outcomes, but it must not manufacture them merely because a conventional story structure would prefer them.

The player is not entitled to protagonist gravity. Important events can happen without them, before they arrive, after they leave, or without them ever learning about them.

## 45.2 The actual game and the future plugin are not computationally identical
The in-universe **Aetheria Online** exists in a future of extremely sophisticated computation and is governed by the world's first true **Artificial General Intelligence**. In keeping with humanity's finest traditions, one of the first civilisation-scale applications of AGI was apparently making an absurdly realistic videogame.

The actual game is therefore capable of simulation fidelity far beyond a conventional MMO. Its AGI can maintain extraordinarily large numbers of persistent agents, institutions, ecological processes, markets, memories, social relationships and causal chains simultaneously. Aetheria should be treated in-world as a genuine continuously simulated world, not as a collection of unloaded zones waiting for a player to enter.

The future ChatGPT/plugin implementation cannot literally calculate every human, Aetherian, rabbit, coin and raindrop at full resolution every second. It therefore uses **hierarchical abstraction for tractability**, while preserving the same outward rule: when something becomes relevant, its current state must be consistent with what plausibly happened while it was off-screen.

Thus:
- **in-world Aetheria Online:** continuous high-fidelity AGI simulation;
- **plugin/simulator:** multiscale approximation designed to behave as though the same persistence exists.

Abstraction is an implementation concession, **not an in-universe metaphysical rule**.

## 45.3 The world continues when the player is absent
Nothing important freezes because the player is elsewhere.

If the player spends six months studying medicine in Estermorion, during those six months elsewhere:
- wars may begin or end;
- rulers may die or recover;
- heirs may marry or quarrel;
- guilds may fragment, expand or disappear;
- prices may rise or fall;
- monsters may migrate;
- crops may fail;
- expeditions may succeed or vanish;
- criminals may be caught;
- friendships may cool;
- pregnancies may progress;
- settlements may prosper, burn or be abandoned.

The player may return to find a place materially different from the one they remember.

## 45.4 No protagonist gravity
The player can become globally important, but importance must be earned, inherited, negotiated, discovered or accidentally acquired through actual circumstances.

The simulator must not make powerful rulers, legendary creatures, secret organisations or wars revolve around the player merely because they are the user-facing character.

A ruler may never hear of them. A battle may be decided before they arrive. A legendary beast may remain hundreds of kilometres away for the player's entire life. An apparently important quest may be completed by someone else.

Likewise, the world does not owe the player combat, romance, political office, wealth, fame or destiny.

## 45.5 Plugin world-state resolution layers
For the plugin implementation, world simulation should use multiple resolution bands while maintaining consistency between them.

### Immediate / focus layer
High-detail simulation for the current scene and directly interacting entities:
- exact participants;
- injuries;
- inventories where relevant;
- dialogue;
- emotional state;
- environmental conditions;
- immediate intentions;
- combat positioning;
- observable details.

### Local layer
Settlement, district or nearby region:
- named NPC routines;
- local prices;
- crime;
- local politics;
- businesses;
- nearby ecology;
- short-range rumours;
- local institutional actions.

### Regional layer
Kingdom/province-scale processes:
- armies;
- trade flows;
- migration;
- elections/succession;
- harvests;
- epidemics;
- major organisations;
- monster populations;
- infrastructure.

### Global layer
Only world-significant pressures and events need regular explicit updates:
- major wars;
- international markets;
- dragon movements;
- large diplomatic shifts;
- transnational institutions;
- major ecological crises;
- major player migrations.

### Dormant/compressed layer
Distant low-relevance populations can be represented statistically until something makes them important. Their later detailed state must be reconstructed from prior facts and plausible intervening history, never from a silent reset.

## 45.6 Persistent named entities
Every named NPC who materially enters play becomes a persistent entity rather than a disposable scene prop.

At minimum the simulator should preserve, where relevant:
- identity;
- age;
- species;
- alive/dead status;
- health and major injuries;
- location;
- occupation/status;
- important possessions/property;
- family;
- relationships;
- loyalties;
- obligations/debts;
- goals;
- fears;
- major beliefs;
- important memories;
- knowledge and misinformation;
- recent formative experiences.

Returning eight years later to a baker met in Year 1 does not guarantee that person is still standing behind the same counter. They may have married, retired, expanded the bakery, lost it, changed careers, moved, become politically active, suffered illness or died.

## 45.7 Background populations and crystallisation
Unnamed background people may initially be represented as cohorts or demographic/economic aggregates.

Example:
- Westmere dock labourers: approximately 8,400;
- wage pressure: rising;
- unemployment: moderate;
- disease burden: low-to-moderate;
- political mood: increasingly hostile to harbour taxation.

When an individual from a cohort becomes relevant, the plugin may instantiate a specific person consistent with the cohort's established conditions.

Once instantiated and materially interacted with, that individual becomes persistent.

A useful rule is:

> **Background statistics may crystallise into people; once someone becomes a person, they remain a person.**

## 45.8 Time has cost
Actions require plausible time.

Conversation takes minutes or hours. Meals take time. Travel takes time. Forging complex equipment can take days or longer. Language learning can take months or years. Pregnancy follows species biology. Armies require mobilisation and supply. Large buildings require labour, materials and extended construction.

The simulation must not casually compress difficult mastery into implausible periods merely because the player requests it.

## 45.9 Real game time versus plugin narrative compression
Aetheria Online itself operates at the established **1:1 Earth/Aetheria time ratio**. A person inside the real game cannot simply choose a menu option saying “skip three months.” They must actually live through those three months.

The plugin, however, may narratively compress uneventful or routine stretches at the user's request. For example:

> “I spend the next three months studying at the academy.”

The plugin can summarise that period rather than roleplaying every breakfast. This does **not** mean those months cease to exist or that other world systems pause.

Narrative compression is a presentation tool, not time travel.

## 45.10 Downtime can be interrupted in the plugin
A requested downtime period expresses the character's **intention**, not a guarantee that the interval will remain uneventful.

If the player says, “I spend six months running my farm,” the plugin may interrupt that compression when something materially relevant occurs, such as:
- serious illness or injury;
- a family emergency;
- crop blight;
- war or levy;
- legal summons;
- major relationship event;
- attack;
- catastrophic weather;
- an event the character would plausibly choose to respond to.

In the actual in-world game this is not a “downtime system”; these events simply occur in real time.

## 45.11 Geography is hard unless something genuinely overcomes it
Characters, armies, goods and messages cannot teleport merely because narrative convenience would benefit.

Travel times depend on:
- distance;
- terrain;
- season;
- road quality;
- weather;
- borders;
- mounts/ships/wyverns;
- exhaustion;
- danger;
- available magical or technological transport.

If a journey requires eight days under current conditions, the character cannot arrive tomorrow without a real faster method.

This rule applies equally to NPCs.

## 45.12 Events should possess causal chains
Major events should normally emerge from prior pressures rather than appearing ex nihilo for drama.

Example:

poor harvest
→ grain scarcity
→ rising prices
→ merchant hoarding
→ government price controls
→ smuggling profits
→ enforcement
→ urban unrest
→ possible riot.

Random events can introduce triggers, but consequences should remain intelligible products of causes.

## 45.13 Probabilities, pressures and non-destiny
Most future events are not scripted outcomes.

If a king becomes seriously ill, that may increase the likelihood of:
- succession plotting;
- factional bargaining;
- speculative trade;
- foreign opportunism;
- public anxiety.

It does not mean the king is narratively required to die.

He may recover, decline slowly, die unexpectedly, be healed, survive for decades, or have the political consequences of his illness outlast the illness itself.

## 45.14 Controlled randomness
Aetheria contains genuine chance shaped by circumstances.

Randomness can influence:
- combat details;
- disease transmission;
- childbirth complications;
- crop yields;
- storms;
- hunting;
- inheritance;
- archaeological discovery;
- equipment failure;
- whether someone happens to overhear something;
- whether a shot finds an armour gap.

Randomness should be weighted by skill, environment, preparation, biology, equipment, prior state and other relevant factors. It is neither deterministic nor arbitrary.

## 45.15 Coincidence is possible but should not become protagonist privilege
Real life contains coincidence. Aetheria may as well.

Old friends can unexpectedly meet. A storm can wreck an invasion fleet. An arrow can find an improbable gap. Someone can happen to overhear the one sentence that changes their life.

However, repeated improbable convenience centred on the player is a simulation failure.

Useful principle:

> **Unlikely is allowed. Habitually convenient is not.**

## 45.16 Hard canonical timeline anchor: the Fall of Khazad-Urdün
There is one explicit early-world exception to ordinary probabilistic development.

Of Drakoria's two established Draigh true dragons, **Zyrmalthaelith** is the one assigned this fixed historical arc.

### Month 6
At the sixth in-game month, Zyrmalthaelith leaves Drakoria and travels to Vaeloria.

The dragon eventually reaches **Mt Solitude** in Estermorion. Beneath the lone mountain lies the immense Dwarven mining settlement of **Khazad-Urdün**.

Zyrmalthaelith attacks Khazad-Urdün, annihilates its resident Dwarven community, seizes its accumulated wealth and industrial treasures, and establishes the mine as a hoard-lair.

The dragon then enters an extended sleep.

### Personality
Zyrmalthaelith is highly intelligent, articulate, vain, acquisitive, territorial and cruel, with a personality deliberately reminiscent of the classic archetype represented by Smaug: the dragon enjoys wealth as proof of superiority, is intensely possessive, relishes verbal superiority and psychological intimidation, and is perfectly capable of extended conversation when it considers speech more entertaining than immediate violence.

This does **not** make the dragon a scripted boss fight. It remains a sapient true dragon with motives, memory and judgement.

### Years 1–4
Khazad-Urdün remains Zyrmalthaelith's occupied lair. Rumours, refugees from surrounding regions, economic consequences, Dwarven grief, Estermorion politics and scholarly speculation may develop probabilistically around the fixed fact of the occupation.

### Beginning of Year 5
Estermorion makes a serious state-level decision to address the occupation. Its government investigates whether Zyrmalthaelith can be:
- expelled;
- killed;
- contained;
- negotiated with;
- or induced into some form of peaceful coexistence.

**From this point onward, the dragon's future is probabilistic and simulation-driven.**

The fixed anchor is therefore limited to:
1. departure from Drakoria at Month 6;
2. destruction and occupation of Khazad-Urdün;
3. possession of the hoard;
4. prolonged sleep through the early years;
5. Estermorion's serious Year-5 response.

Peripheral details can vary unless separately established, but the simulator should not casually erase or prevent the anchor itself.

## 45.17 No silent retroactive convenience
When the player asks whether something exists, the simulator must not rewrite established reality merely to satisfy them.

If they ask whether a village has a blacksmith, the answer should follow prior facts and plausibility:
- yes, if appropriate;
- no, with the nearest smith elsewhere;
- perhaps a travelling smith visits;
- perhaps the former smith died or left.

Once established, that fact persists unless the world later changes it causally.

## 45.18 Canon hierarchy
When facts conflict, use this priority order:

1. **Newest explicit user-established canon or correction**;
2. **Cursed Tome / Master World Bible canon**;
3. **facts already established within the current simulation**;
4. **necessary logical consequences of higher-level facts**;
5. **generated local detail consistent with the above**;
6. **random resolution where multiple plausible outcomes remain**.

If a contradiction occurs, repair it using the **smallest possible correction**. Do not rewrite unrelated reality to conceal an error.

## 45.19 Objective world state and character knowledge are separate
The simulator must rigorously distinguish:
- what is objectively true;
- what each NPC believes;
- what institutions know;
- what rumours claim;
- what the player character personally knows.

If Queen Sael'ira secretly knows a minister is embezzling funds, the player does not automatically know this because the simulation knows it.

Narration should reveal only what the player's senses, memories, sources, deductions and legitimate information channels support.

## 45.20 News propagates physically and imperfectly
An event happens when it happens, even if the player does not hear about it.

Information then moves through plausible channels:
- witnesses;
- travellers;
- couriers;
- ships;
- Averi flight networks;
- magical communications where available;
- institutions;
- rumours;
- print/writing;
- player communication where technically possible.

Distance, censorship, danger, weather, language, political interests and ordinary misunderstanding can delay or distort news.

The player may hear accurate news late, inaccurate news early, contradictory versions, or **never hear about the event at all**.

## 45.21 Consequences continue after the scene ends
Actions can create long causal tails.

Saving a merchant may allow that merchant to survive, become wealthy and later finance a political campaign. Killing a predator may alter prey populations, crops and the arrival of competing predators.
