Not every minor action produces a civilisation-changing butterfly effect. Most consequences attenuate. But the engine should preserve significant causal residue rather than resetting once the player leaves.

## 45.22 Goals do not guarantee achievement
The simulator supports mundane or nonheroic lives, but even these are not wish fulfilment.

A player may spend twenty years trying to become the greatest baker in Aetheria. They may become excellent, successful and famous. They may also discover another baker is objectively more talented, experienced, innovative or technically skilled.

Likewise, wanting to be the greatest swordsman, richest merchant, most beloved ruler or finest physician does not create a hidden protagonist bonus.

The simulator supports the **pursuit** of goals; it does not guarantee supremacy.

## 45.23 Core update loop for the plugin
For each meaningful turn or time advance, the plugin should conceptually perform the following sequence:

1. determine elapsed in-world time;
2. resolve the player's declared action or intention;
3. update directly affected entities and environment;
4. resolve queued local events whose trigger conditions are met;
5. advance relevant off-screen institutions, relationships, economies, ecology and political processes;
6. apply hard canonical anchor events when their dates/conditions arrive;
7. propagate information through plausible channels;
8. update what the player actually knows;
9. persist new named entities, facts, injuries, relationships, debts, deaths and material consequences;
10. narrate only what is observable or reasonably knowable.

This loop should prefer causal continuity over convenient plotting.

## 45.24 IV.A endpoint
IV.A establishes Aetheria as a **persistent causal world rather than a stage set**.

The actual fictional game achieves this through AGI-level continuous simulation. The future plugin approximates it through hierarchical state resolution, persistent entities, causal event queues, realistic time/geography, hidden-state separation, weighted randomness, knowledge propagation and strict canon precedence.

The principal rule remains:

> **Simulation first. Narrative second. Continuity always.**

**IV.A — Persistent World State, Time & Causality is complete.**

---


# 46. IV.B — NPC / Institution Decision-Making & Social Simulation

## 46.1 Foundational principle
Aetherian people are not dialogue trees, reputation meters, or bundles of fixed utility values. Every meaningful individual acts from an **internal perspective** shaped by personality, temperament, values, beliefs, emotions, relationships, social position, culture, incentives, obligations, memory, incomplete information, and current circumstances.

Institutions are likewise not hive minds. Their behaviour emerges from rules, culture, personnel, leadership, factions, precedent, assets, public reputation, material interests, and external pressures.

The cardinal social-simulation rule is:

> **Every person and institution acts from its own internal perspective, not from the player's narrative role.**

A friend may refuse the player. An enemy may respect them. A lover may leave them. A subordinate may disobey. A bureaucracy may lose their paperwork. A ruler may make a terrible emotional decision. A foolish ruler may accidentally choose correctly. No relationship or institution exists merely to facilitate the player's story.

## 46.2 Personality architecture: tendencies, not sliders
NPC personality is represented as a network of **interacting tendencies** rather than a single set of rigid scalar traits such as “Manipulation 92” or “Bravery 74.” Useful dimensions can include, without being limited to:
- baseline temperament;
- social confidence;
- patience;
- empathy;
- pride;
- curiosity;
- aggression;
- conscientiousness;
- need for control;
- tolerance for ambiguity;
- attachment style;
- humour;
- shame sensitivity;
- status sensitivity;
- ideological rigidity;
- capacity for self-restraint;
- habitual conflict style.

These tendencies are **context-sensitive**. A person may be patient in diplomacy, impulsive with family, ruthless toward enemies, generous to dependants, and sentimental about one private subject without being inconsistent.

The simulator should therefore avoid flattening complex people into one adjective or alignment category.

## 46.3 Stable personality, but not immutable personality
Core temperament usually changes gradually through age and accumulated experience.

However, **life-changing events can produce rapid personality change** when the causal pressure is sufficiently severe. Examples include:
- catastrophic trauma;
- near-death experiences;
- profound betrayal;
- sudden bereavement;
- sustained abuse;
- religious revelation or crisis;
- becoming a parent;
- severe illness;
- sudden acquisition or loss of great power;
- a transformative romantic relationship;
- witnessing an atrocity;
- being responsible for a disaster;
- magical or soul-level injury.

Rapid change must still be psychologically causal. The simulator should not rewrite a personality merely because three conversations with the player were pleasant.

## 46.4 Emotional state as active decision pressure
NPCs maintain active emotional states such as anger, fear, affection, joy, shame, grief, jealousy, hope, anxiety, excitement, exhaustion, humiliation, resentment, relief, desire, guilt, and disgust.

Emotion affects:
- attention;
- memory retrieval;
- risk assessment;
- willingness to compromise;
- interpretation of ambiguous behaviour;
- impulse control;
- social openness;
- decision speed;
- likelihood of regrettable action.

Emotion does **not** replace personality or reasoning. A normally cautious person may make a reckless choice while furious and later regret it. A proud person may temporarily swallow humiliation when terrified. A grieving ruler may become indecisive without suddenly becoming unintelligent.

The simulator should model emotions as pressures and filters rather than binary mind-control states.

## 46.5 Emotional memory
People remember both events and the emotional residue those events leave.

A person may forget the exact wording of an insult but remember humiliation. A decades-old act of kindness may retain powerful emotional significance after details blur. Conversely, factual memory of an event can survive after the original emotional intensity has faded.

Important memories can therefore contain:
- factual content;
- confidence in that content;
- emotional valence;
- emotional intensity;
- associated person/place/context;
- later reinterpretations.

Memory is imperfect, and different people can remember the same event differently.

## 46.6 Relationship matrices are directional
All meaningful relationships are **asymmetric** and tracked separately in each direction.

A may love B while B merely likes A. A may trust B's competence while disliking B personally. B may resent A while remaining loyal. A subordinate can fear a commander, respect their ability, dislike their personality, and still trust them with their life.

No universal mutual “relationship score” exists.

## 46.7 Relationship dimensions
Important relationships should be represented through multiple interacting dimensions, such as:
- affection;
- trust;
- respect;
- fear;
- attraction;
- familiarity;
- obligation;
- dependency;
- admiration;
- resentment;
- jealousy;
- pity;
- gratitude;
- perceived reliability;
- ideological compatibility;
- shared identity;
- shared history.

These dimensions can conflict. High affection does not guarantee trust. Attraction does not guarantee respect. Obligation can keep enemies cooperating. Familiarity can deepen love or contempt.

## 46.8 Romance remains emergent and non-deterministic
Romance follows the previously established rules. Attraction depends on:
- orientation;
- physical preferences;
- personality preferences;
- chemistry;
- familiarity;
- values;
- social status and role;
- circumstance;
- perceived safety;
- shared experiences;
- cultural expectations;
- individual quirks.

No repeated-gift mechanic guarantees romance. There is no hidden dialogue path that turns a permanent “no” into “yes.”

Political marriage may occur without attraction. Attraction can exist without compatibility. Love can exist without a successful relationship. Rejection is possible and can remain final.

## 46.9 Self-models can be wrong
NPCs do not possess perfect introspection.

A person may sincerely misidentify their own motive:
- believing principle drives them when resentment is also decisive;
- believing they are in love when they are lonely or dependent;
- believing they are fearless while repeatedly avoiding danger;
- believing they seek justice while partly seeking revenge;
- believing they are unaffected by status while constantly reacting to it.

The simulator may maintain deeper motivational pressures than the character consciously recognises. Narration should reveal these only through behaviour, contradiction, confession, inference, or appropriate perspective—not through omniscient exposition.

## 46.10 Bounded rationality and belief-state decision-making
People choose among the options **they believe exist**, not the options objectively available in the world.

Decision quality depends on:
- information accuracy;
- intelligence and expertise;
- time available;
- emotional state;
- trust in sources;
- cognitive bias;
- cultural assumptions;
- available resources;
- perceived consequences.

A brilliant general can make a disastrous decision because scouts were wrong. A merchant can reject a favourable deal because they believe it fraudulent. A healer can choose the wrong treatment because a symptom was concealed.

Intelligence improves reasoning. It does not grant omniscience.

## 46.11 Biases and heuristics
NPCs can possess culturally and individually learned shortcuts, including:
- stereotypes;
- prejudice;
- professional assumptions;
- class expectations;
- superstition;
- confirmation bias;
- sunk-cost reasoning;
- availability bias;
- loyalty bias;
- deference to authority;
- suspicion of outsiders;
- overconfidence from past success.

Biases are not universal and are not automatically permanent. They may weaken through contradictory experience, strengthen through propaganda or trauma, or persist despite obvious exceptions.

The simulator should avoid giving every member of a culture an identical prejudice.

## 46.12 Values and material interests are separate pressures
The simulator distinguishes between **what benefits someone materially** and **what they believe is right or meaningful**.

A noble can support reforms that reduce personal wealth because they sincerely believe the reforms are just. A merchant can sacrifice profit to protect family reputation. A ruler can pursue an economically damaging war because of honour, grief, ideology, nationalism, religion, or spite.

Material incentives matter greatly, but “follow the money” is not a universal explanation of human behaviour.

## 46.13 Risk tolerance is domain-specific
There is no single universal “risk-taking” score.

A person may be:
- financially reckless but physically cautious;
- romantically bold but politically conservative;
- fearless in battle but unwilling to gamble;
- professionally experimental but socially timid.

Risk assessment should depend on the domain, perceived stakes, expertise, emotion, and personal history.

## 46.14 Social masks and role-specific selves
Many people—especially leaders, courtiers, merchants, clergy, diplomats, criminals, and celebrities—maintain different public and private presentations.

Possible layers include:
- public persona;
- professional persona;
- familial self;
- intimate/private self;
- secret self-image.

For example, Queen Sael'ira may display elegant composure at court, calculated warmth with diplomats, irritation with her sister, and private uncertainty she would never reveal publicly.

Maintaining a social mask can itself create fatigue, stress, resentment, loneliness, or identity confusion.

## 46.15 Promises, debts, oaths and obligations
Meaningful commitments are tracked with different strengths and contexts.

These include:
- sacred oaths;
- written contracts;
- sworn fealty;
- family duties;
- professional obligations;
- debts;
- favours owed;
- promises;
- implied expectations;
- hospitality duties;
- political patronage.

Breaking an obligation depends on personality, stakes, law, reputation, competing duties, perceived legitimacy, and the expected consequences.

Not all promises are equal, and competing obligations can create genuine moral dilemmas.

## 46.16 NPC initiative
NPCs actively pursue goals without waiting for player input.

They may:
- write first;
- seek a meeting;
- propose marriage;
- request help;
- offer employment;
- make peace;
- start a feud;
- expose a secret;
- sabotage a rival;
- flee;
- relocate;
- form or end relationships;
- demand repayment;
- bring a lawsuit;
- join an institution;
- resign from one;
- recruit allies;
- act against the player's interests.

The player does not have to press an “advance relationship” button for other people to have agency.

## 46.17 Decision cycle for an individual NPC
When a meaningful NPC faces a consequential choice, the simulator should conceptually evaluate:

1. **Perceived situation** — what does this person think is happening?
2. **Current goals** — what are they trying to achieve now and long-term?
3. **Values and taboos** — what will they refuse or strongly prefer on principle?
4. **Relationships** — who matters to them and how?
5. **Obligations** — what promises, debts, roles or duties constrain them?
6. **Emotions** — what immediate feelings are altering judgement?
7. **Resources and authority** — what can they actually do?
8. **Believed options** — what choices do they think are available?
9. **Risk by domain** — which options feel dangerous to this person?
10. **Biases and habits** — what shortcuts distort evaluation?
11. **Expected consequences** — what do they think will happen?
12. **Noise/chance** — uncertainty, hesitation, miscommunication, timing.
13. **Choice** — select the action most consistent with the combined pressures, not automatically the objectively optimal action.
14. **Aftermath** — update emotion, memory, relationships, reputation, obligations and future goals.

This process should be weighted and contextual rather than mechanically exposing numerical scores to the player.

## 46.18 Institutional identity exceeds leadership
Institutions persist beyond individual officeholders.

Institutional behaviour emerges from:
- constitutions, charters, laws and formal procedure;
- institutional culture;
- precedent;
- permanent officials and staff;
- leadership;
- factions and patronage networks;
- property and financial interests;
- archives and institutional memory;
- public expectations;
- professional norms;
- external allies and rivals;
- material capacity.

Replacing a ruler, guildmaster, director, archmagister, or commander can substantially alter direction, but does not automatically rewrite everyone beneath them.

## 46.19 Institutional inertia
The difficulty of changing an organisation scales with factors such as:
- size;
- age;
- geographic spread;
- procedural complexity;
- legal constraints;
- professional culture;
- factional resistance;
- implementation costs;
- reliance on outside partners.

A five-person guild can rewrite its constitution over lunch.

Ferrum Argentarium changing an accounting standard across continents may take years even if senior leadership unanimously approves it.

An absolute monarch may issue an order instantly while discovering that implementation still requires governors, clerks, money, messengers, enforcement, and cooperation.

## 46.20 Factions emerge from causes, not drama quotas
Institutions are not automatically divided into a neat fixed number of factions.

Factions arise when groups share:
- ideology;
- material interests;
- grievances;
- identities;
- patronage relationships;
- professional outlooks;
- regional interests;
- succession preferences;
- security concerns;
- religious commitments.

A highly disciplined organisation may remain unusually cohesive. Another institution may contain dozens of overlapping blocs. A person can belong to several factional networks at once.

Factions can weaken or disappear when the issue sustaining them disappears.

## 46.21 Collective decisions require procedures
When an institution “decides” something, the simulator should identify **who can actually decide and through what process**.

Examples:
- Estermorion may require legislation, cabinet action, administrative implementation, judicial review, or some combination;
- a monarch may personally decide policy but still depend on ministers and officials to execute it;
- a merchant house may require partner consent or family hierarchy;
- a tribal confederacy may seek consensus among chiefs;
- a player guild may use founder authority, council vote, referendum, or delegated command;
- bureaucrats may reinterpret or quietly dilute implementation.

“Country X decides Y” is therefore shorthand for an actual institutional process, never a hive-mind action.

## 46.22 Institutional decision cycle
For consequential organisational action, the simulator should conceptually resolve:

1. define the issue as the institution understands it;
2. identify the legally/formally empowered decision-makers;
3. identify relevant staff, factions, stakeholders and outside pressures;
4. establish information available to each actor;
5. model leadership preferences and factional incentives;
6. apply procedural rules and precedent;
7. account for institutional culture and inertia;
8. determine the formal decision;
9. determine how faithfully the bureaucracy or membership implements it;
10. model resistance, evasion, delay, corruption, competence and resource constraints;
11. propagate public and elite reactions;
12. update institutional reputation, factional strength, precedent and future policy space.

Thus **decision** and **implementation** are separate stages.

## 46.23 Institutions remember
Institutional memory persists through:
- archives;
- contracts;
- precedent;
- veteran staff;
- traditions;
- procedures;
- training;
- grudges;
- professional norms;
- infrastructure designed around previous crises.

An organisation can remember something after everyone directly involved has died.

Conversely, poor archives, purges, disasters, corruption or political revisionism can cause institutions to forget, distort or suppress their own history.

## 46.24 Social consequences are not player-centred
The simulator must allow outcomes that are socially inconvenient to the player without converting them into hidden quests.

Examples:
- a person permanently rejects romance;
- a friend chooses another duty over the player;
- an employer refuses a request;
- a ruler will not grant an audience;
- a relative disagrees and never changes their mind;
- an institution's procedure cannot be bypassed merely because the player is famous;
- a rival is promoted because they were genuinely better qualified.

There is no requirement that every refusal contains a route to eventual player success.

## 46.25 IV.B endpoint
IV.B establishes that Aetheria's social world is generated from **agents with partial knowledge, conflicting motives, persistent relationships, emotional continuity, imperfect self-understanding and real institutional constraints**.

The simulator should produce social drama as an emergent consequence of people wanting different things—not as a quota imposed from above.

The governing rule remains:

> **People are agents, not content. Institutions are systems, not personalities.**

**IV.B — NPC / Institution Decision-Making & Social Simulation is complete.**

---


# 47. IV.C — Economy, Politics, War, Ecology & Large-Scale Systems

## 47.1 Core principle: systems, not single scores
Aetheria's large-scale simulation does not reduce economies, governments, armies, or ecosystems to one decisive number. Outcomes emerge from **interacting systems, bottlenecks, feedback loops, trade-offs, institutions, geography, material resources, human behaviour, and chance**.

A wealthy state can lose a war. A huge army can starve. A poor polity can be extraordinarily difficult to conquer. A stable dictatorship can outlast a richer but fragmented rival. A democracy can make disastrous choices. Advanced technical knowledge can remain useless without the infrastructure required to implement it.

For the fictional AGI-run Aetheria Online, these systems can be simulated at far greater fidelity than the eventual GPT/plugin. The plugin should approximate the same logic through persistent regional/system states and causal updates rather than pretending the world is a set of isolated encounters.

## 47.2 Prices emerge from supply, demand, and friction
Important prices are dynamic rather than fixed RPG shop values.

They respond to:
- local production;
- harvest quality;
- population and consumption;
- inventories and stockpiles;
- war and mobilisation;
- taxation and tariffs;
- transport costs and distance;
- piracy and banditry;
- monster activity;
- labour availability;
- speculation and hoarding;
- monopoly or cartel behaviour;
- player demand;
- regulation;
- access to credit;
- seasonal variation.

A sword, sack of grain, horse, healing reagent, house, or day's labour can therefore have radically different prices in different regions and at different times.

## 47.3 Barter remains economically important
Coinage is widespread but not universal.

The existing currency canon remains authoritative, including:
- Argents;
- Charter Coins;
- Olive Coins;
- Northern Coins;
- Viper Coins;
- High Coins;
- Arpak Coins;
- Raid Coins;
- Choreleth Coins;
- Dragon Coins;
- Lavender Coins;
- regional coinages not yet individually detailed.

However, some polities and communities rely heavily on **barter, raw materials, weighed metal, livestock, favours, reciprocal obligation, labour, or commodity exchange**.

This is especially relevant in places already established as lacking standardised coinage, including Grralrrogrra, Rralarror, and the Suralian Isles, though barter can occur anywhere when liquidity collapses or trust in currency fails.

## 47.4 Arbitrage is real but self-eroding
If salt is cheap in one region and expensive in another, merchants and players can profit by moving it.

Repeated arbitrage then changes the opportunity:
1. traders notice the margin;
2. supply increases at the expensive destination;
3. source prices may rise;
4. destination prices may fall;
5. governments may impose taxes or regulation;
6. competitors enter;
7. bandits, pirates, and protection rackets notice the richer route;
8. insurers and guards adjust prices;
9. infrastructure may improve if the route becomes strategically important.

Profitable routes therefore evolve rather than functioning as infinite money exploits.

## 47.5 Goods are physically real
Economically important goods exist materially in the world.

If a warehouse records 10,000 sacks of wheat, approximately 10,000 sacks must actually exist somewhere unless the records are fraudulent.

Consequences include:
- warehouses can burn;
- grain can rot;
- animals require food and care;
- ships have finite cargo capacity;
- mines can be exhausted, flooded, occupied, or cut off;
- armies physically consume food and equipment;
- wagons and pack animals matter;
- theft requires taking or falsifying control over actual assets;
- destruction in war removes real productive capital rather than merely reducing a number.

## 47.6 Currency and exchange rates
Currencies can fluctuate relative to one another according to:
- metal content;
- issuing authority credibility;
- military and political stability;
- regional demand;
- convertibility;
- counterfeiting risk;
- transport and assay costs;
- banking confidence;
- wartime debasement;
- merchant preference.

Ferrum Argentarium strongly supports the international role of Argents and provides exchange, assay, credit, and settlement services, but it does not magically fix all exchange rates or eliminate local monetary crises.

Bank runs, liquidity crises, coin shortages, debasement scares, counterfeit panics, and sovereign defaults are all possible.

## 47.7 Banking, credit, and debt
Debt is a persistent obligation, not a temporary quest flag.

Loans can involve:
- interest;
- collateral;
- guarantors;
- repayment schedules;
- legal jurisdiction;
- credit reputation;
- restructuring;
- seizure of pledged assets;
- litigation;
- negotiated settlement;
- debtor flight;
- imprisonment where local law permits it;
- political disputes when states themselves are debtors.

Banks evaluate risk before lending. Obvious lunatics do not receive unlimited unsecured capital merely because they are players.

Ferrum is especially formidable at enforcing valid obligations across jurisdictions through legal networks, commercial pressure, investigators, correspondent institutions, and reputation. Crossing a border or respawning pre-Lock does not erase a debt.

## 47.8 Labour markets and coercive labour systems
Wages and working conditions respond to labour scarcity, skill scarcity, migration, disease, war, law, discrimination, guild regulation, and bargaining power.

Examples:
- plague reducing farm labour can raise surviving workers' wages;
- a player migration into one city can crash low-skilled wages;
- translators, soul-healers, physicians, engineers, navigators, and other scarce experts can command large premiums;
- guild monopolies can restrict entry;
- discriminatory law can suppress mobility;
- rural depopulation can create land opportunities while destroying local production.

However, ordinary wage-market logic is heavily distorted in much of **Choreleth**, where slavery or other coercive labour arrangements are widespread in several states. Zhabura, Peromia, Keralfidar, Jelorbidur, Gor'oxuer'funro, and other coercive systems can suppress wages, redirect labour by force, and produce economic outputs that cannot be understood through free labour markets alone.

This does not make coerced labour economically frictionless: resistance, supervision costs, flight, sabotage, mortality, skill shortages, rebellion risk, and low incentives can still materially affect productivity.

## 47.9 Technology requires an enabling ecosystem
Earth knowledge does not bypass material reality.

Introducing a major technology generally requires some combination of:
- correct conceptual knowledge;
- suitable raw materials;
- precision tooling;
- tolerances and measurement standards;
- skilled workers;
- reliable energy sources;
- maintenance knowledge;
- spare parts;
- capital;
- transport infrastructure;
- institutional support;
- demand sufficient to sustain production.

Knowing how a steam engine works does not create an industrial revolution overnight. Once the supporting ecosystem exists, however, technology can diffuse rapidly and transform productivity, warfare, transport, medicine, and politics.

## 47.10 Magic is a scarce economic input
Magic can transform productivity without functioning as infinite free energy.

Magical work is constrained by:
- soul strain;
- training time;
- aptitude;
- fatigue;
- specialist availability;
- materials or reagents where relevant;
- legal regulation;
- risk;
- opportunity cost.

A skilled mage may dramatically accelerate healing, construction, detection, transport, agriculture, or manufacturing, but one mage cannot service unlimited demand.

Magic therefore creates sectoral comparative advantages and specialised labour markets rather than abolishing economics.

## 47.11 State capacity is multidimensional
There is no single 'state strength' statistic.

Governments can vary independently in their ability to:
- assess and collect taxes;
- keep records;
- enforce law;
- mobilise armies;
- maintain roads and ports;
- conduct censuses;
- regulate markets;
- gather intelligence;
- administer welfare or relief;
- manage public health;
- communicate orders;
- coordinate between regions;
- suppress corruption;
- sustain legitimacy.

A tribal confederacy may mobilise warriors brilliantly while struggling with permanent bureaucratic record-keeping. A surveillance dictatorship may gather enormous quantities of information while producing terrible analysis because officials fear reporting bad news.

## 47.12 States require revenue
Rulers and governments cannot fund armies, fleets, courts, roads, fortifications, hospitals, bureaucracies, palaces, relief programmes, or diplomacy without resources.

Revenue may come from:
- land taxes;
- customs and tariffs;
- harbour dues;
- market fees;
- mining rights;
- crown or state estates;
- monopolies;
- tribute;
- borrowing;
- confiscation;
- religious contributions;
- licence fees;
- extraordinary wartime levies.

Excessive taxation can generate avoidance, smuggling, capital flight, unrest, reduced investment, rebellion, or contraction. Insufficient taxation can leave a state incapable of implementing its own policies.

## 47.13 Political instability emerges structurally
Coups, revolutions, civil wars, separatism, and regime collapse emerge from interacting pressures rather than a random 'rebellion meter'.

Potential drivers include:
- succession disputes;
- unpaid troops;
- famine or food inflation;
- elite factionalism;
- regional grievance;
- ideological conflict;
- legitimacy crises;
- charismatic claimants;
- foreign backing;
- military defeat;
- tax pressure;
- corruption;
- repression;
- state weakness;
- religious conflict;
- demographic or economic shocks.

An unpopular ruler can survive for decades when elites, army, bureaucracy, treasury, and coercive institutions remain cohesive. A popular ruler can still lose control if the state disintegrates underneath them.

## 47.14 Diplomacy follows interests, constraints, and credibility
International relations are not governed by friendship meters.

States and rulers consider:
- security threats;
- geography;
- trade;
- resources;
- historical grievance;
- ideology;
- dynastic ties;
- domestic politics;
- alliance credibility;
- personal ruler relationships;
- military readiness;
- treaty precedent;
- economic dependence;
- third-party threats.

Two monarchs can loathe one another personally while maintaining a durable alliance. Two close friends can preside over states whose interests are drifting toward war.

## 47.15 War requires mobilisation
Armies do not appear instantly when war is declared.

Mobilisation can require:
- political authorisation;
- messengers;
- mustering levies or professionals;
- mercenary negotiations;
- gathering mounts and draft animals;
- equipment repair;
- ammunition and supply preparation;
- medical arrangements;
- route planning;
- intelligence;
- transport contracts;
- naval assembly where relevant.

This creates meaningful windows for surprise, deterrence, pre-emption, diplomacy, and strategic deception.

## 47.16 Logistics matter enormously—but do not replace strategy and tactics
Logistics can decide wars because armies require food, water, fodder, pay, medicine, ammunition, replacement equipment, roads, bridges, transport, and communication.

An army too large for its terrain can be weaker than a smaller force because it consumes itself.

Cutting a supply line can destroy a campaign without winning a major battle.

However, logistics are **not the only thing that matters**. The actual 'fun part' of war remains central:
- strategy;
- operational manoeuvre;
- battlefield tactics;
- terrain use;
- intelligence;
- deception;
- command quality;
- timing;
- morale;
- combined arms;
- magical capabilities;
- individual martial skill;
- fortifications;
- initiative;
- chance.

A well-supplied army can still be outmanoeuvred or annihilated in battle. A brilliant tactical victory can save a failing campaign. A genius operational commander may exploit an enemy logistics weakness that another commander would never notice.

Aetherian war is therefore neither 'logistics simulator only' nor 'heroic battle decides everything'. Both layers interact.

## 47.17 Morale and discipline
Military formations are composed of people, not RTS units.

Performance is affected by:
- hunger;
- exhaustion;
- disease;
- fear;
- confidence in commanders;
- unit cohesion;
- training;
- discipline;
- pay;
- ideological commitment;
- home concerns;
- recent victories or defeats;
- rumours;
- battlefield shock.

Elite units can hold under conditions that break inexperienced troops, but no formation is infinitely immune to human limits.

## 47.18 Sieges are logistical, political, and military crises
Fortified positions can require prolonged sieges rather than short combat encounters.

Attackers may use:
- blockade;
- starvation;
- bombardment;
- mining;
- magical assault;
- direct storming;
- sabotage;
- bribery;
- betrayal;
- disease;
- psychological warfare.

Defenders must manage:
- food;
- clean water;
- disease;
- fire;
- civilian morale;
- political factionalism;
- ammunition;
- repairs;
- medical care;
- sanitation;
- relief expectations.

Sieges can last months or years. Magic changes the technical possibilities but does not eliminate the need to feed people, maintain defences, gather intelligence, and hold morale together.

## 47.19 War reshapes society after the fighting
War produces long causal tails.

Possible consequences include:
- refugees;
- amputees and disabled veterans;
- widows and orphans;
- abandoned farms;
- destroyed infrastructure;
- sovereign debt;
- inflation;
- labour shortages;
- veteran political movements;
- banditry;
- disease spread;
- demographic imbalance;
- radicalisation;
- border disputes;
- revenge cycles;
- land redistribution;
- technological diffusion.

A military victor can emerge economically poorer and politically less stable than the defeated state.

## 47.20 Ecology is part of civilisation
Ecology is directly coupled to politics and economics.

Examples:
- predator extermination can cause herbivore booms and crop loss;
- abandoned wartime farmland can rewild and attract predators or monsters;
- overhunting can destroy local food systems;
- monster migration can close roads and alter trade;
- river modification can improve irrigation while destroying fisheries;
- forest clearance can produce erosion and flooding;
- invasive species can transform agriculture and public health;
- protecting an apex predator can anger livestock owners while stabilising an ecosystem.

There is no separate 'wildlife minigame'. Ecological decisions can become fiscal, military, agricultural, and political issues.

## 47.21 Systemic shocks
Rare shocks can be civilisation-shaping when geography and conditions permit.

Possible events include:
- drought;
- flood;
- earthquake;
- volcanic eruption;
- severe winter;
- epidemic;
- crop blight;
- magical contamination;
- monster migration;
- wildfire;
- urban fire;
- bank panic;
- infrastructure collapse;
- shipping disaster.

They are generated from plausible conditions and probabilities, not because the player has gone too long without excitement.

The same shock can produce wildly different outcomes depending on institutional competence, reserves, infrastructure, leadership, social trust, geography, and sheer luck.

## 47.22 Feedback loops and bottlenecks
Large systems should often behave through reinforcing and balancing feedback.

Example negative spiral:
war -> reduced planting -> poor harvest -> grain inflation -> unrest -> harsher taxation -> evasion -> lower revenue -> unpaid army -> desertion -> weaker security -> banditry -> even worse trade.

Example stabilising response:
poor harvest -> government releases grain reserves -> prices moderate -> emergency imports arrive -> unrest remains contained -> tax relief protects farmers -> next year's planting recovers.

Bottlenecks can dominate otherwise powerful systems. A state may have money but no ships, soldiers but no fodder, iron but no charcoal, mages but no trained healers, food but no transport capacity.

## 47.23 Large-scale simulation architecture for the plugin
The eventual plugin should represent large-scale systems through persistent regional and institutional state rather than attempting impossible full-resolution simulation of every transaction.

Useful tracked categories include:
- population and demographic pressure;
- food supply and reserves;
- key commodity availability;
- labour supply;
- wage pressure;
- trade-route condition;
- transport capacity;
- fiscal health;
- debt and credit stress;
- military readiness;
- mobilisation status;
- morale;
- political legitimacy;
- factional tension;
- administrative capacity;
- ecological pressure;
- disease burden;
- disaster exposure;
- infrastructure condition;
- magical specialist availability.

These are not single determinant scores. They are compact summaries used to produce causal interactions consistent with the full fictional AGI simulation.

## 47.24 IV.C cardinal rule
The governing rule is:

> **No large-scale outcome may be decided by one number alone.**

Systems interact through material constraints, institutions, geography, human choices, ecological pressures, information, chance, and feedback loops.

Earth knowledge can become revolutionary only when people can actually build, finance, operate, maintain, distribute, and socially absorb what that knowledge proposes.

War can be decided by supply, strategy, tactics, politics, morale, terrain, intelligence, technology, individual brilliance, institutional failure, or combinations of all of them.

The simulation should therefore ask not merely **'Who is stronger?'** but **'Stronger in what way, under which conditions, for how long, at what cost, and with what bottleneck?'**

**IV.C — Economy, Politics, War, Ecology & Large-Scale Systems is complete.**


# 48. IV.D — Encounters, Combat, Risk & Resolution

## 48.1 Resolution begins with intent, not menu commands
Combat actions are interpreted from the actor's stated intention and the actual physical situation.

A player can say simply:
> “I attack him.”

The simulator can resolve a plausible attack from stance, weapon, range, skill and circumstances. More technically meaningful description can matter when it supplies a real tactical idea:
> “I feint high, step outside his sword arm, then thrust toward the armpit as he raises his guard.”

Detailed input is not rewarded merely for sounding technical. It matters only when the described mechanics are physically or magically relevant and the character is capable of attempting them.

## 48.2 Resolution math remains hidden
The player ordinarily does not see numerical hit chances, armour penetration percentages, injury rolls or artificial health totals.

The simulator narrates consequences in-world:
- a shield intercepts the blow;
- the point skids across plate;
- footing collapses in mud;
- a spear enters an exposed gap;
- the target twists just far enough to turn a lethal thrust into a glancing wound.

The residual/ghost HUD may still display legitimately known skills, traits, status information and certain conditions, but it is not a combat spreadsheet projected over reality.

## 48.3 Skill and physical capability are separate variables
Combat ability emerges from multiple interacting factors, including:
- technical training;
- weapon familiarity;
- strength;
- speed;
- reach;
- body mass;
- reflexes;
- coordination;
- endurance;
- flexibility;
- sensory ability;
- species anatomy;
- current injury and fatigue.

Skill can compensate dramatically for physical disadvantage, but it does not abolish physics. A smaller master can defeat a larger novice; a half-tonne Golem that lands a successful body-check still carries half a tonne of momentum.

## 48.4 Weapons possess real mechanical characteristics
Weapons are not primarily bundles of damage numbers.

Relevant properties include:
- reach;
- mass and balance;
- edge geometry;
- point geometry;
- leverage;
- hand protection;
- recovery time after a committed motion;
- suitability in confined spaces;
- armour interaction;
- formation use;
- durability;
- ammunition requirements;
- mounted or two-handed use.

Thus spears, swords, axes, maces, daggers, bows, polearms and improvised weapons solve different problems.

Players inevitably design **hybrid weapons** attempting to combine advantages. Some become genuinely useful innovations. Many are awkward, structurally weak, badly balanced or dangerous to their wielder. The simulation judges them by actual construction and use rather than rewarding novelty.

## 48.5 Armour protects where armour exists
Armour is resolved through material, construction, fit, geometry and physical coverage.

A breastplate does not protect the face merely because the wearer has a high abstract armour statistic.

Different forms behave differently:
- mail is exceptionally effective against many cuts but does not make blunt trauma irrelevant;
- plate can deflect or stop attacks while preserving vulnerable articulations and gaps;
- padding can reduce blunt force and improve armour performance;
- helmets trade protection against hearing, visibility, heat and comfort to varying degrees;
- poor fit can reduce mobility and create new vulnerabilities.

Armour can make a trained combatant genuinely difficult to kill rather than merely increasing the number of wounds they can absorb.

## 48.6 Living bodies do not possess literal hit-point biology
For ordinary living beings, damage is anatomical.

Relevant structures include:
- skin;
- muscle;
- tendons and ligaments;
- bones and joints;
- arteries and veins;
- peripheral nerves;
- lungs;
- abdominal organs;
- heart;
- brain and spinal structures;
- species-specific organs;
- magical organs where applicable.

Twenty shallow cuts can be survivable while one well-placed injury can be fatal. Massive or magical creatures survive greater trauma because their bodies, scale, redundancy, regeneration, unusual tissues or metaphysics permit it—not because a hidden health bar grants arbitrary durability.

## 48.7 Bleeding, shock and delayed death matter
A wound can be immediately survivable yet progressively lethal.

Major risks include:
- arterial haemorrhage;
- venous bleeding;
- internal bleeding;
- loss of circulating volume;
- traumatic shock;
- organ failure;
- respiratory compromise;
- later infection.

A fighter may win and then die minutes later without treatment. Combat medicine, first aid, evacuation, surgery and magical healing can therefore decide survival after the fighting appears finished.

## 48.8 Injuries impair function before death
Combatants do not remain at full performance until a threshold suddenly reaches zero.

Examples:
- damaged tendon -> weak or lost grip;
- hand fracture -> poor weapon control;
- leg wound -> reduced mobility or collapse;
- broken ribs -> painful breathing and reduced endurance;
- concussion -> confusion, nausea, delayed reaction and poor judgement;
- eye injury -> reduced visual field/depth perception;
- nerve injury -> numbness, weakness or paralysis.

Adrenaline can suppress pain awareness temporarily. It cannot make a severed tendon mechanically functional.

## 48.9 Pain, fear and adrenaline are real combat pressures
Pain is neither a universal stun effect nor irrelevant flavour.

Its impact varies with:
- injury type;
- individual tolerance;
- training;
- expectation;
- adrenaline;
- species physiology;
- exhaustion;
- psychological state.

Fear can alter reaction, attention, decision-making, fine motor control and willingness to continue. Training and discipline can mitigate these effects without making anyone psychologically invulnerable.

## 48.10 Fatigue is physical, not a dodge-roll meter
Combat can become exhausting very quickly.

Fatigue depends on:
- conditioning;
- armour and carried mass;
- weapon use;
- heat/cold;
- terrain;
- hydration;
- injuries;
- stress;
- breathing efficiency;
- species physiology;
- duration and intensity of exertion.

An experienced fighter who manages distance and effort can defeat an equally strong opponent who attacks wildly and exhausts themselves.

## 48.11 Martial skill is granular
There is no single universal **Combat 87** competence.

A person can separately vary in:
- spear work;
- swordsmanship;
- grappling;
- archery;
- mounted combat;
- formation fighting;
- shield use;
- battlefield awareness;
- duelling;
- fighting multiple opponents;
- armour use;
- command under pressure;
- reading feints;
- unfamiliar-weapon adaptation.

A tournament champion and a battlefield veteran may both be elite while possessing significantly different strengths.

## 48.12 Awareness and surprise can dominate a fight
Combat begins before the first swing.

Relevant factors include:
- line of sight;
- sound;
- lighting;
- cover;
- concealment;
- camouflage;
- scent where species can use it;
- distraction;
- magical detection;
- watch discipline;
- prior expectation of danger.

An extraordinary fighter can die to an attack they never perceive. Conversely, excellent awareness can prevent an ambush from ever achieving surprise.

## 48.13 Ranged attacks obey plausible trajectories
Arrows, bolts, thrown weapons and technological projectiles are resolved through their physical and magical characteristics.

Relevant factors include:
- range;
- launch energy;
- projectile mass and shape;
- wind;
- gravity/trajectory;
- target movement;
- obstruction;
- shooter stability;
- weapon condition;
- armour interaction;
- training.

The narration need not perform visible physics equations, but impossible shots do not become possible because the player describes them confidently.

## 48.14 Grappling is a major part of close combat
Close combat can include:
- clinching;
- wrestling;
- throws;
- trips;
- pins;
- joint control;
- weapon trapping;
- disarms;
- ground fighting.

This is especially important against armour. A heavily armoured opponent may be far easier to kill by disrupting balance, controlling them and attacking a gap than by attempting to cut through their strongest protection.

Strength and mass matter heavily in grappling, but leverage, timing and technical skill can compensate substantially.

## 48.15 Magic follows established metaphysics, not a universal physics-only rule
Magic does **not** need to behave as disguised conventional physics.

Established canon already includes:
- objective souls;
- soul strain;
- soul-healing;
- memory and cognition magic;
- psychic domination/interference;
- wards and structural magic;
- counter-magic;
- curses;
- magically generated or enhanced physical phenomena;
- forbidden soul, time and reality practices.

Therefore magic is resolved according to the **specific magical mechanism involved**.

Some magic creates ordinary physical consequences and then those consequences interact normally with matter: a magically accelerated stone still possesses momentum; magically produced flame can transfer heat; a ward physically intercepting a projectile changes its trajectory or stops it according to the ward's properties.

Other effects operate directly on magical, mental or soul structures and need not reduce cleanly to conventional physics. Memory magic, soul-healing, psychic control and curses are examples.

Magic remains constrained by factors such as:
- aptitude;
- learned technique;
- soul strain;
- fatigue;
- concentration;
- target resistance;
- counter-magic/wards;
- preparation;
- range and line of effect where relevant;
- reagents/materials where relevant;
- legal/ethical restrictions;
- the particular spell or tradition's rules.

Healing magic repairs biological and/or soul damage through its established mechanism. It is not a generic **+HP** effect and cannot be assumed to reverse every injury equally easily.

## 48.16 Group combat is not a queue of duels
Multiple attackers act simultaneously and attempt to support one another.

Numbers matter because attackers can:
- threaten from several directions;
- constrain movement;
- rotate tired fighters;
- grapple;
- use missile support;
- exploit distraction.

However, more bodies can also create:
- crowding;
- blocked attack lanes;
- friendly-fire risk;
- command problems;
- poor footing;
- inability to exploit numerical superiority in confined terrain.

A narrow doorway may allow one excellent defender to survive against numbers that would destroy them in open ground. Named characters do not receive cinematic turn-taking privileges.

## 48.17 Morale, surrender and flight are normal
Sapient opponents ordinarily possess self-preservation.

Depending on personality, discipline and circumstance, combatants may:
- retreat;
- rout;
- surrender;
- negotiate;
- freeze;
- feign surrender;
- fight harder when cornered;
- continue for ideological or personal reasons.

Animals and monsters act according to ecology, temperament, pain, territory and hunger rather than human honour codes.

How a victor treats surrendering or fleeing enemies can create legal, political and reputational consequences.

## 48.18 Nonlethal violence is not automatically safe
There is no universal Hollywood **knockout button**.

Head trauma, strangulation, blunt-force injury and chemical sedation can all disable someone and can also kill or permanently injure them.

Safer capture is more likely through:
- skilled restraint;
- grappling;
- specialised equipment;
- carefully dosed medicine;
- appropriately designed magical effects;
- overwhelming control without unnecessary injury.

Aetherian magic can include **reliable incapacitating effects** when the user actually knows an appropriate spell/technique and the target is susceptible. Psychic/somnolent, paralytic or related magic is possible within established metaphysics, and rare biological-magical effects such as paralytic wyvern mist already exist. Such effects are not universal, resistance-free or automatically harmless; wards, species physiology, dose/power, target condition, counter-magic and soul/mental strain can all matter.

No previously established canon requires a single universal named “Sleep” spell, so IV.D does not invent one as a default tool. Specific incapacitation spells can be defined when a magical tradition actually teaches them.

## 48.19 No encounter scaling
Aetheria does not rebalance danger around the player.

A new player entering a true dragon's lair encounters the same dragon that an elite coalition would.

Conversely, ordinary robbers do not spontaneously become legendary assassins because the player has become powerful.

Some encounters are unfair because reality can be unfair. Good judgement includes recognising when the correct action is to flee, negotiate, hide, surrender, seek allies or avoid the encounter entirely.

## 48.20 Hybrid weapons and experimental gear
Player and NPC inventors can combine weapon concepts, magical components and technologies.

A hybrid design succeeds only if the combined system remains mechanically coherent. Evaluation considers:
- structural strength;
- balance;
- accidental self-contact;
- moving-part reliability;
- ergonomic control;
- magical interference;
- maintenance;
- ammunition/reagent supply;
- training demands;
- whether the supposed advantages actually coexist in practice.

A bizarre weapon can become brilliant. It can also become a device whose statistically most endangered person is its owner.

## 48.21 Resolution pipeline
For a meaningful combat exchange, the plugin should conceptually resolve:

1. **Intent** — what each actor is trying to accomplish.
2. **Knowledge/awareness** — what each actor knows and perceives.
3. **Positioning** — distance, angles, terrain, cover, footing, formation.
4. **Timing** — initiative in the physical sense: who can act, interrupt, recover or exploit an opening.
5. **Capability** — relevant skill, body, species traits, equipment, magic, current condition.
6. **Action and defence** — attack, evade, parry, block, counter, grapple, spell, flee, surrender, etc.
7. **Contact** — whether and where an action actually lands.
8. **Material/magical interaction** — armour, shields, wards, weapons, spell mechanics, obstacles.
9. **Anatomical/system effect** — tissue, structure, magical organ, soul or other target-specific damage/effect.
10. **Immediate consequences** — bleeding, pain, shock, impaired movement, disarm, knockdown, magical strain, fear.
11. **Morale and decision update** — continue, retreat, surrender, change tactics, panic, protect someone, etc.
12. **Next exchange** — repeat from the new state rather than resetting to neutral positions.
13. **Aftermath** — treatment, capture, death, recovery, legal consequences, equipment damage and persistent injury.

The simulator need not expose this checklist mechanically in narration. It is an internal causal discipline.

## 48.22 Probability without narrative immunity
Controlled randomness can decide close or uncertain outcomes, but probabilities are shaped by all relevant circumstances.

A novice can land a fortunate lethal strike. An expert should defeat a novice overwhelmingly often under favourable conditions, but not because the universe preselects the expert as winner.

Named status, fame, protagonist relevance or narrative importance confer **no automatic survival bonus**.

## 48.23 IV.D cardinal rule
The governing rule is:

> **Combat is resolved from physical circumstance, skill, intent, information, psychology, magic and probability—not from narrative importance.**

The simulator must never decide the winner first and then fabricate a plausible-looking exchange to justify that result.

It resolves the interaction from the current state, allows consequences to change the next state, and accepts the outcome that emerges.

A famous hero can die to an unlucky spear. A frightened novice can accidentally kill someone far better trained. A heavily outnumbered veteran can exploit terrain brilliantly. A stronger fighter can lose because they are exhausted, surprised or overconfident.

The resulting fight becomes compelling because its drama emerges from **cause and consequence**, not because the world secretly protects whichever combatant seems more narratively important.

**IV.D — Encounters, Combat, Risk & Resolution is complete.**


# 49. IV.E — Information, Memory, Rumours & Knowledge

## 49.1 Information is part of world state
Objective reality and believed reality are separate simulation layers.

A fact can be objectively true while:
- nobody knows it;
- only a few witnesses know it;
- most people believe the opposite;
- the official record is wrong;
- the truth is known but not believed;
- accurate information is trapped behind a language, class, geographic, political, or institutional boundary.

The reverse is equally important. A king may already be dead while large parts of the world sincerely believe he is still alive because the news has not reached them, because the court is concealing the death, or because earlier reports of illness have not yet been superseded.

The engine therefore treats **knowledge distribution** as a real part of persistent world state rather than assuming facts become universally known when they occur.

## 49.2 Facts, claims, beliefs, and evidence are distinct
For simulation purposes, important information can be represented through four related but non-identical layers:
- **objective fact** — what actually happened;
- **claim** — what someone says happened;
- **belief** — what a person or institution currently accepts as likely true;
- **evidence** — what can be used to justify, verify, challenge, or legally establish a claim.

A claim can be true without persuasive evidence. A false claim can be supported by misleading evidence. A person can know something happened yet be unable to prove it to anyone else.

This distinction is essential for law, scholarship, espionage, politics, religion, journalism, reputation, and ordinary social life.

## 49.3 Knowledge distribution
For sufficiently important facts, the simulator may track categories such as:
- direct witnesses;
- people informed by witnesses;
- people holding documentary or magical evidence;
- people with partial knowledge;
- people who suspect the truth;
- people who believe a false alternative;
- people who have heard nothing;
- people whose information is outdated.

Knowledge need not be universal even among members of the same institution. A minister can know something the monarch does not. A field commander can know something headquarters has not yet received. A family can conceal something from a village for years.

## 49.4 Provenance matters, but provenance is not proof
People normally care where information came from.

Possible provenance includes:
- personal observation;
- family or friends;
- a trusted professional;
- a court proclamation;
- a newspaper or public notice;
- a guild report;
- a priest or religious authority;
- a traveller;
- a sailor;
- a drunk stranger;
- an anonymous letter;
- a magical recording;
- a confessed participant;
- an enemy source;
- hearsay with no traceable origin.

However, provenance is not mechanically decisive.

A person may readily believe:
> “My brother got married last weekend.”

because the claim is ordinary, socially plausible, and comes from someone likely to know.

The same listener may demand extraordinary evidence for:
> “Elania personally appeared to me and declared that I am her chosen mortal.”

Even if the speaker sincerely claims direct divine revelation.

The simulator should therefore evaluate credibility from **source reliability + claim plausibility + stakes + available corroboration + prior beliefs + evidence**, rather than treating “I saw it myself” or “a trusted person told me” as automatic proof.

Extraordinary claims can be true. They simply do not become socially persuasive merely because the claimant says so.

## 49.5 Confidence is separate from truth
Belief strength is not accuracy.

A person can be:
- correct and highly confident;
- correct but doubtful;
- wrong and uncertain;
- wrong and absolutely convinced.

Confidence can reflect:
- source trust;
- emotional investment;
- ideology;
- expertise;
- repeated exposure;
- apparent corroboration;
- social pressure;
- overconfidence;
- propaganda;
- personal experience.

The loudest person in the tavern may still be entirely wrong.

## 49.6 Memory is reconstructive, not a perfect recording
Ordinary people do not store flawless transcripts of experience.

Memory retention is affected by:
- time;
- salience;
- emotional intensity;
- repetition and rehearsal;
- age;
- sleep;
- intoxication;
- stress;
- trauma;
- illness;
- neurological or magical damage;
- individual mnemonic ability;
- later information.

People can retain the gist of an event while losing details. Someone may remember being publicly humiliated for decades while forgetting the exact insult.

The simulator should avoid both extremes:
- perfect lifelong memory for everyone;
- cartoonishly unreliable memory where nothing can be trusted.

## 49.7 False and reconstructed memories
Memory can become inaccurate through ordinary cognition without deliberate deception.

Possible distortions include:
- combining two similar events;
- assigning a remembered statement to the wrong speaker;
- remembering a later rumour as though it had been known at the time;
- unconsciously improving one's own role in a story;
- confusing repeated retellings with direct experience;
- remembering the wrong face, date, route, colour, or sequence;
- filling missing details with plausible assumptions.

A confident memory is therefore evidence, not metaphysical certainty.

Magical interference can make memory distortion far more severe, but ordinary false memory exists without magic.

## 49.8 Emotional memories are sticky
Highly emotional experiences tend to persist more strongly than mundane ones.

Examples include:
- terror;
- grief;
- humiliation;
- falling in love;
- betrayal;
- triumph;
- life-threatening danger;
- religious experience;
- profound shame;
- witnessing atrocity.

However, emotional persistence does not guarantee perfect factual accuracy. A person may remember exactly how terrified they were while misremembering which direction an attacker approached from.

This interacts with IV.B's emotional-memory rule: relationships can remain shaped by the emotional residue of events whose exact wording has faded.

## 49.9 Rumours mutate in transmission
Rumours are not static data packets.

At each retelling, information can undergo:
- omission;
- compression;
- exaggeration;
- simplification;
- accidental substitution;
- moral interpretation;
- cultural reframing;
- translation loss;
- sensational embellishment;
- conflation with another event;
- deliberate alteration.

A possible chain:

> “King Harlan has taken ill.”
> → “The King is seriously ill.”
> → “The King is dying.”
> → “The King has died.”
> → “The King was murdered.”
> → “An Elven sorceress poisoned the King.”

Nobody in that chain necessarily began by lying.

## 49.10 Information travels through networks
Different kinds of information spread through different channels.

Important networks include:
- family and kinship;
- taverns and inns;
- merchants and caravaners;
- sailors and ports;
- military units;
- clergy and temples;
- royal and government couriers;
- universities and scholarly correspondence;
- guild communication;
- criminal networks;
- noble households;
- rural markets;
- player organisations;
- diplomatic missions;
- Whitewing Relay and comparable institutions where relevant.

A rumour can become common knowledge in one network while remaining unknown in another population living nearby.

Social position therefore affects information access.

## 49.11 News has travel time
Events do not automatically become known when they occur.

Information speed depends on:
- geographic distance;
- roads and terrain;
- ships and currents;
- weather;
- borders and war;
- literacy;
- courier infrastructure;
- magical communication where available;
- Averi aerial travel or other specialised transport;
- deliberate censorship;
- whether anyone considers the information worth transmitting.

A battle can be objectively over while distant commanders still issue orders based on a world that ceased to exist days ago.

Some events may never reach a given character at all.

## 49.12 Literacy and record cultures differ
Aetherian societies vary in how information is preserved and transmitted.

Differences can include:
- general literacy;
- elite literacy;
- bureaucratic paperwork;
- newspapers or gazette-like publications;
- religious manuscripts;
- commercial records;
- private letters;
- legal registries;
- oral historians;
- genealogists;
- memorised law;
- songs, poems, and public recitation;
- magical recording traditions.

A strongly oral culture is not automatically less historically sophisticated than a literate one. It may instead use specialised custodians, formulaic preservation, public correction, ritual repetition, or other mechanisms for maintaining continuity.

Likewise, a paperwork-heavy state can preserve enormous quantities of nonsense.

## 49.13 Archives are power
Records can determine what society is able to establish as legitimate.

Archives may contain evidence concerning:
- land ownership;
- inheritance;
- citizenship;
- taxation;
- debt;
- noble descent;
- marriage;
- criminal history;
- magical licensing;
- military service;
- treaties;
- borders;
- census data;
- contracts;
- court judgments.

Destroying, stealing, falsifying, restricting, or selectively publishing archives can therefore have immense political consequences.

Objective history does not change when a document burns. What society can **prove** may change completely.

A person may truly own a field and still lose the legal dispute because the only surviving deed was destroyed.

## 49.14 Evidence, law, and investigation
Courts and investigators do not gain access to simulator truth.

They operate from available evidence:
- testimony;
- physical traces;
- documentary records;
- forensic medicine;
- magical evidence where admissible;
- motive;
- opportunity;
- confession;
- circumstantial patterns;
- expert interpretation.

Therefore:
- a guilty person can be acquitted;
- an innocent person can be convicted;
- a case can remain unresolved;
- later evidence can overturn an earlier conclusion.

The engine may know who committed a murder while every living investigator reaches the wrong answer.

## 49.15 Lies and deception
Deception depends on more than a single charisma statistic.

Relevant factors include:
- what the liar actually knows;
- whether the lie is rehearsed;
- emotional control;
- consistency with known facts;
- audience expectations;
- prior trust;
- evidence;
- skill at performance;
- interrogation pressure;
- stakes;
- cultural norms;
- magical detection or countermeasures.

There is no universal visible **LIE DETECTED** signal.

Specific magical techniques may detect aspects of deception, emotional state, memory, intent, or cognition if established by their own rules, but no generic ability should automatically convert subjective testimony into perfect truth.

## 49.16 Propaganda and public belief
Governments, religious institutions, guilds, businesses, criminals, rebels, and individuals can deliberately shape public understanding.

Methods include:
- proclamations;
- sermons;
- patriotic songs;
- monuments;
- school curricula;
- censorship;
- selective publication;
- statistical framing;
- forged documents;
- controlled ceremonies;
- planted rumours;
- sponsored storytellers;
- scapegoating;
- symbolic trials;
- staged displays of competence or piety.

Propaganda usually works best when it attaches itself to:
- an existing fear;
- an existing prejudice;
- a desired identity;
- a familiar story;
- a plausible grievance;
- something the audience already wants to believe.

Repeated messaging can increase familiarity and confidence without increasing truth.

## 49.17 Reputation is distributed information
There is no universal reputation score.

Reputation is a collection of beliefs held by particular people and networks.

The same person can be:
- celebrated as a hero in Westmere;
- unknown in Eshv'alara;
- despised by one guild;
- respected by an enemy military;
- considered a fraud by scholars;
- falsely reported dead in Narthol.

Reputation travels, mutates, and can lag behind current behaviour.

A person can also become trapped by an old reputation long after they have changed.

## 49.18 Secrets are knowledge graphs
A secret is not merely a hidden Boolean variable.

For important secrets, the simulator should conceptually track:
- who knows the full truth;
- who knows part of it;
- who suspects it;
- who possesses evidence;
- who was told but does not believe it;
- who has been deliberately misled;
- who knows that someone else knows;
- what records survive.

If all direct holders die and all evidence is destroyed, knowledge can genuinely disappear from the living world.

The underlying historical fact remains true, but it may cease to be recoverable without later rediscovery.

## 49.19 Lost knowledge can be rediscovered
Information loss need not be permanent.

Knowledge can return through:
- archaeology;
- archival discovery;
- surviving testimony;
- magical investigation;
- scientific experimentation;
- linguistic reconstruction;
- comparative scholarship;
- accidental rediscovery;
- deduction from material consequences.

Rediscovered knowledge may be incomplete or interpreted differently from how the original people understood it.

A later culture can recover the correct fact while misunderstanding its meaning.

## 49.20 Memory and psychic magic do not create omniscience
Existing magical canon remains controlling.

Memory, cognition, psychic, soul, recording, and related magic operate according to the actual technique used.

General constraints include:
- the spell can access or alter only what its mechanism reaches;
- a remembered experience is not automatically objective truth;
- false memories can be magically reinforced;
- psychic intrusion can be resisted, detected, warded, or legally restricted depending on method and jurisdiction;
- soul damage can impair memory and cognition;
- magical recordings can be partial, edited, forged, misinterpreted, or stripped of context where the relevant magic permits;
- extraordinary magical expertise does not make a person universally omniscient.

The existence of Seli'ara Ithraeven and other memory specialists demonstrates that magical cognition can be extraordinarily powerful, but specialist power remains bounded by magical rules, resistance, knowledge, ethics, and circumstance.

## 49.21 Player knowledge and character knowledge are separate
For the eventual plugin, information shown to the human user does not automatically become actionable knowledge for every character they control.

If the user has seen information through:
- another character;
- an earlier point-of-view scene;
- GM-facing exposition;
- a prior campaign;
- an omniscient world summary;

then a current character may still lack that information.

The simulator should prevent convenient metagaming unless the character has a plausible route to the knowledge.

If the user deliberately directs a character to act on unknown information, the simulator should interpret only what the character can plausibly attempt, not retroactively grant them knowledge.

## 49.22 Information lifecycle
A useful conceptual pipeline is:

> **fact/event → witness/source → claim → transmission channel → delay → distortion → recipient interpretation → confidence → memory → retelling/recording → later verification, mutation, or loss**

At every stage, the information can diverge from objective reality.

The AGI-run in-world game can model this continuously across enormous populations. The plugin approximates the same principle by preserving important knowledge states, source relationships, communication delays, rumours, records, and belief differences.

## 49.23 Cardinal information rule
The governing rule of IV.E is:

> **No character may know something merely because the simulator knows it. Information must reach them through a plausible channel.**

Its companion rule is:

> **What a character believes may be wrong, incomplete, outdated, manipulated, or internally inconsistent.**

A player can therefore make a rational decision from inaccurate information and suffer real consequences without the simulation having cheated.

Likewise, the truth can exist for years without any relevant character discovering it.

That is not missing exposition.

That is a functioning world.

---

# 50. Phase IV.F — Emergent Events, Continuity & GM Safeguards

Phase IV.F defines how Aetheria produces new developments without devolving into constant manufactured drama, and how the eventual plugin preserves continuity over extremely long simulations.

The fictional in-world version of Aetheria Online is administered by an AGI capable of maintaining world state at enormous scale. The plugin cannot literally hold billions of people, objects, institutions and ecological processes in immediate context. Its task is therefore to reproduce the **behavioural consequences** of that world through disciplined persistence, causal event generation, state ledgers and canon safeguards.

The central principle is that Aetheria must remain a world rather than a sequence of scenes manufactured for the protagonist.

## 50.1 Quiet is a valid world state
The simulation is not required to generate an attack, quest, revelation, romance beat, betrayal, monster encounter, political crisis or mystery merely because several turns have been uneventful.

A day may contain:
- breakfast;
- ordinary work;
- a walk;
- routine conversation;
- shopping;
- study;
- rain;
- sleep.

If no strong causal pressure produces a dramatic event, then **nothing especially dramatic happening is a correct simulation result**.

The plugin must never maintain an invisible entertainment meter that periodically forces danger or drama into the player's vicinity.

## 50.2 Existing pressures come before newly invented events
When determining what happens next, the engine should first inspect conditions already in motion.

Examples include:
- a predator ranging near livestock;
- a deteriorating marriage;
- unpaid soldiers;
- a crop disease already spreading;
- a storm approaching along an established weather pattern;
- a creditor whose repayment date is nearing;
- a pregnant person approaching labour;
- an investigation gathering evidence;
- a festival already scheduled;
- a rival considering retaliation;
- an army already mobilising;
- a political faction preparing a vote.

Only after existing causal threads are considered should the simulator introduce genuinely new stochastic events.

This creates continuity: the world preferentially **develops what is already happening** rather than spawning a parade of unrelated plots.

## 50.3 Event horizons
Events and trends exist on different natural timescales. The simulator should respect those timescales rather than compressing every development into the next few turns.

Useful horizons include:
- **immediate:** seconds to hours;
- **short:** days to weeks;
- **medium:** months to years;
- **long:** years to generations.

Examples:
- an ambush may resolve in seconds;
- an infection may develop over days;
- a succession crisis may evolve over months;
- a language shift may require generations.

Processes can accelerate when conditions genuinely justify acceleration, but the simulator may not fast-forward causality solely because the user is paying attention to the issue.

## 50.4 Unresolved threads remain alive
A meaningful unresolved matter remains part of world state even when the user stops mentioning it.

Examples:
- a debt remains due;
- a threat remains remembered;
- a pregnancy continues;
- a court case proceeds;
- an expedition continues travelling;
- a promised visit remains a social obligation;
- a wound continues healing or worsening;
- a siege continues consuming supplies.

The simulator should not treat conversational silence as cancellation.

A pregnancy does not remain at seven months for three years because the player never asked about the pregnant person again.

## 50.5 Threads may resolve without the player
Unresolved developments can conclude off-screen through the actions of other people and systems.

A feud may end through:
- marriage;
- mediation;
- murder;
- legal settlement;
- economic exhaustion;
- war;
- generational succession;
- one side simply losing interest.

The player may return years later and discover that a once-important problem was settled without them.

This is not lost content. It is anti-protagonist-gravity functioning correctly.

## 50.6 Persistent identity anchors
Every important named entity should receive a durable identity anchor once instantiated.

For a person this can include:
- canonical name and aliases;
- species;
- sex/body where relevant;
- approximate or exact age;
- date of birth if established;
- current location;
- occupation/title/status;
- family and relationships;
- major loyalties;
- health and injuries;
- possessions of importance;
- major experiences;
- knowledge/beliefs;
- obligations;
- alive/dead/resurrected state.

Places, organisations, animals, artifacts and other persistent entities receive equivalent identity bundles appropriate to their type.

Once instantiated, the simulator must not silently replace an entity with a contradictory version merely because earlier details have fallen out of immediate conversational context.

## 50.7 Death is a hard state, but not an absolute terminal state
Death must be explicitly recorded. A dead person cannot casually reappear alive because the simulator forgot the death.

However, Aetheria already possesses **true soul-resurrection**. True resurrection requires returning the **willing soul**; reanimating a soulless corpse is not resurrection. Forced resurrection remains forbidden soul magic.

Therefore the plugin must distinguish:
- ordinary living state;
- death;
- soulless corpse animation/other undeath;
- true resurrection;
- final story termination.

### Pre-Lock player death
The established pre-Lock respawn system operates normally.

### Post-Lock plugin death choice — explicitly non-diegetic
If the user's player-character dies after Lock-In, the **GPT/plugin simulator**, not Aetheria itself, should break from ordinary narration and present a meta-level continuation choice in approximately this form:

> **You died. Do you wish to resurrect, or end [Character Name]'s story?**

This prompt is an out-of-world simulation affordance for the user. The dead character does **not** see a system menu in the Afterlife, VRSAGIM does not personally offer the choice, and no equivalent universal in-world resurrection UI exists.

If the user chooses **end the story**, the death remains a permanent campaign fact. The world may continue through descendants, institutions, other characters or a new campaign, but the dead character is not silently restored.

If the user chooses **resurrect**, this is **not** treated as a standard MMO respawn or an immediate undo. The character's willing soul is genuinely returned through Aetheria's resurrection framework. The simulator then advances the world by a **randomly determined but causally plausible interval** and resimulates the intervening history.

The returned character may awaken in a substantially changed historical context:
- a new political age or era may have begun;
- old rulers may be dead and replaced;
- borders may have shifted;
- friends may have aged, died, married or had descendants;
- guilds may have risen or collapsed;
- settlements may have expanded, declined or vanished;
- wars may have begun and ended;
- institutions may remember the character differently from living contemporaries.

The resurrection choice preserves the character's identity; it does **not** freeze the world while they were dead.

The character's exact bodily condition after resurrection follows the established resurrection method rather than automatically restoring a perfect copy of the pre-death body.

## 50.8 Location is hard state
Important characters and objects possess actual locations.

If Queen Sael'ira is in Eshv'alara, she cannot simultaneously attend a meeting in High Hearth unless a real communication/transport mechanism permits it.

If a sword is locked in a chest in another city, the player cannot draw it from their belt.

Changing location requires:
- travel;
- transport;
- magical movement;
- theft;
- shipment;
- deliberate transfer;
- or another established mechanism.

Travel time continues to obey IV.A.

## 50.9 Ownership, possession and believed ownership are distinct
For significant property, the simulator should distinguish at least:
- **legal/recognised ownership**;
- **physical possession/control**;
- **claimed ownership**;
- **believed ownership by relevant observers/institutions**.

A person can legally own a sword while another person borrows it. A thief can physically possess it without owning it. An innocent merchant may buy stolen property while believing title is legitimate. A court may reach yet another conclusion from available records.

The same principle applies to land, animals, ships, titles, documents, bank assets and other important property.

## 50.10 Physical changes persist
The environment and bodies do not reset between scenes.

If:
- a character loses an eye;
- a bridge collapses;
- an inn burns;
- a castle wall is breached;
- a blade chips;
- a wyvern scars a wing;
- a field is flooded;
